\int_{0}^{T}|z^{\overline{I}}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\leq\epsilon\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|\widehat{z}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f
, we bound

\int_{0}^{T}|z^{I}(t)|\cdot|z^{\overline{I}}(t)|\mathrm{d}t\leq\sqrt{2\epsilon}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

by the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality and have

\int_{0}^{T}|z^{I}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\geq(1-5\sqrt{\epsilon})\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

(18)

Because
\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|z^{\overline{I}}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\leq\epsilon\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t
, inequality (
18
) also indicates that

\int_{0}^{T}|z^{I}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\geq(1-6\sqrt{\epsilon})\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|z^{I}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

∎

One useful property of
z^{I}(t)
is that its maximum can be bounded by its average on
[0,T]
.

Claim 7.8
.

\forall t\in[0,T],|z^{I}(t)|\leq 2\sqrt{\Delta T}\cdot\|z^{I}\|_{T}
.

Proof.

From the definition
|z^{I}(t)|
, it is upper bounded by
\int_{f_{0}-\Delta}^{f_{0}+\Delta}|\widehat{z^{I}}(f)|\mathrm{d}f
for any
t\in[0,T]
. On the other hand,

\displaystyle\int_{f_{0}-\Delta}^{f_{0}+\Delta}|\widehat{z}^{I}(f)|\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2\Delta}(\int_{f_{0}-\Delta}^{f_{0}+\Delta}|\widehat{z}^{I}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\Delta}(\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|z^{I}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{\Delta}(\int_{0}^{T}|z^{I}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\Delta T}\|z^{I}\|_{T}.

∎

Claim 7.9
.

Given
\widehat{\beta}=\frac{C_{\beta}}{\Delta\cdot\sqrt{\Delta T}}
with a sufficiently small constant
C_{\beta}
, for any two
\widehat{\beta}
-close samples in
z^{I}(t)
, we have that

\forall\alpha\in[0,T],\penalty\ \forall\beta\in[\widehat{\beta},2\widehat{\beta}],\quad|z^{I}(\alpha)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}-z^{I}(\alpha+\beta)|\leq 0.01\cdot\|z^{I}\|_{T}.

Proof.

From the definition of the Fourier transform, we have

\displaystyle|z^{I}(a+\beta)-z^{I}(a)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \left|\int_{f_{0}-\Delta}^{f_{0}+\Delta}\widehat{z^{I}}(f)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}(fa+f_{0}\beta)}(e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}(f-f_{0})\beta}-1)\mathrm{d}f\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 2\cdot(2\pi\Delta\beta)\cdot\int_{f_{0}-\Delta}^{f_{0}+\Delta}|\widehat{z^{I}}(f)|\mathrm{d}f

by Taylor expansion

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 4\pi\beta\Delta\cdot\sqrt{2\Delta}\left(\int_{f_{0}-\Delta}^{f_{0}+\Delta}|\widehat{z^{I}}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

by Hölder inequality

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 10\pi\widehat{\beta}\Delta\cdot\sqrt{2\Delta}\left(\int_{0}^{T}|{z^{I}}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

by inequality (
18
)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 10^{-2}\|z^{I}\|_{T}.

∎

We consider how to output an
\alpha
such that
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}\approx z(\alpha+\beta)/z(\alpha)
with high probability in the rest of this section.

If we can sample from
z^{I}(t)
, we already know
|z^{I}(\alpha)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}-z^{I}(\alpha+\beta)|\leq 0.01\|z^{I}\|_{T}
from Claim
7.9
. Then it is enough to find any
\alpha
such that
|z^{I}(\alpha)|\geq 0.5\|z^{I}\|_{T}
. From Claim
7.8
, we can take
O(\sqrt{\Delta T})
samples
\big(z^{I}(\alpha),z^{I}(\alpha+\beta)\big)
where each
\alpha
is uniformly sampled from
[0,T]
such that with high probability, the sample
z^{I}(\alpha)
with the largest norm
|z^{I}(\alpha)|
satisfies
|z^{I}(\alpha)|\geq 0.5\|z^{I}\|_{T}
. Then we have
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}\approx z^{I}(\alpha+\beta)/z^{I}(\alpha)
.

Next, we move to
z(t)=z^{I}(t)+z^{\overline{I}}(t)
and plan to output
\alpha\in[0,T]
with probability at least
0.5
such that
|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha)|\leq 0.1|z^{I}(\alpha)|
and
|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha+\beta)|\leq 0.1|z^{I}(\alpha+\beta)|
. Because the “noise”
z^{\overline{I}}(t)
has
\|z^{\overline{I}}(t)\|^{2}_{T}\geq\epsilon\|z^{I}(t)\|^{2}_{T}
for a constant
\epsilon
and the bound
\sqrt{\Delta T}
in Claim
7.8
is a polynomial in
k
, the approach for
z^{I}(t)
cannot guarantee that
z(\alpha+\beta)/z(\alpha)\approx e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}
with probability more than
1/2
.

The key observation is as follows:

Observation 7.10
.

For a sufficiently small
\epsilon
and
\|z^{\overline{I}}\|^{2}_{T}\leq\epsilon\|z\|^{2}_{T}
, let
D_{T}
be the weighted distribution on
[0,T]
according to
|z(t)|^{2}
, i.e.,
D_{T}(t)=\frac{|z(t)|^{2}}{T\|z\|^{2}_{T}}
. If we sample
\alpha\in[0,T]
from the distribution
D_{T}
instead of the uniform distribution on
[0,T]
,
|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha)|\leq 0.01|z^{I}(\alpha)|
with probability
0.9
.

It follows from the fact that

\displaystyle\E_{\alpha\sim D_{T}}\frac{|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha)|^{2}}{|z(\alpha)|^{2}}=\int_{0}^{T}\frac{|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha)|^{2}}{|z(\alpha)|^{2}}\cdot\frac{|z(\alpha)|^{2}}{T\|z\|^{2}_{T}}\mathrm{d}\alpha=\frac{\int_{0}^{T}|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\alpha}{T\|z\|^{2}_{T}}\leq\epsilon.

In Procedure
GetLegal1Sample
, we collect
(\Delta T)^{2}
samples (in expectation)
\big(z(\alpha),z(\alpha+\beta)\big)
in
S_{\heavy}
with
|z(\alpha)|\geq 0.49\|z\|_{T}
and resample one
\alpha
from these samples according to their norm
|z(\alpha)|^{2}+|z(\alpha+\beta)|^{2}
. We show its correctness as follows.

Because we do not know
0.5\|z\|_{T}
, we use
z_{\emp}
to approximate it.

Claim 7.11
.

Procedure
GetEmpirical1Energy
in Algorithm
3
takes
O((T\Delta)^{2})
samples to output
z_{\emp}
such that
z_{\emp}\in[0.8\|z\|_{T},1.2\|z\|_{T}]
with prob. 0.9.

Proof.

We know
z_{\emp}^{2}=\E_{i\in[R_{\est}]}[|z(\alpha_{i})|^{2}]=\E_{i\in[R_{\est}]}[|z^{I}(\alpha_{i})+z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i})|^{2}]
.

Notice that
\E_{i\in[R_{\est}]}[|z^{I}(\alpha_{i})|^{2}]
is in
[0.99\|z^{I}\|_{T},1.01\|z^{I}\|_{T}]
with prob.
0.99
from the Chernoff bound and Claim
7.8
.

At the same time,
\E_{\alpha_{i}}[|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i})|^{2}]=\|z^{\overline{I}}\|^{2}_{T}
. With prob. 0.92,
\E_{i\in[R_{\est}]}[|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i})|^{2}]\leq 13\|z^{\overline{I}}\|^{2}_{T}
. For a sufficiently small
\epsilon
and
\|z^{\overline{I}}\|^{2}_{T}\leq\epsilon\|z^{I}\|^{2}_{T}
,
\E_{i\in[R_{\est}]}[|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i})|^{2}]\leq 13\epsilon\|z^{I}\|^{2}_{T}
.

At last, we bound the cross terms of
|z^{I}(\alpha_{i})+z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i})|^{2}
by the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality,

\displaystyle\E_{i\in R_{\est}}[|\overline{z}^{I}(\alpha_{i})z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i})|+|z^{I}(\alpha_{i})\overline{z}^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i})|]

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle 2\E_{i\in R_{\est}}[|z^{I}(\alpha_{i})|\cdot|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i})|]

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle 2\left(\E_{i\in[R_{\est}]}[|z^{I}(\alpha_{i})|^{2}]\cdot\E_{i\in[R_{\est}]}[|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i})|^{2}]\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle 10\sqrt{\epsilon}\|z^{I}\|^{2}_{T}.

For a sufficiently small
\epsilon
, we have
\E_{i\in[R_{\est}]}[|z(\alpha_{i})|^{2}]^{1/2}
is in
[0.9\|z^{I}\|_{T},1.1\|z^{I}\|_{T}]
, which is also in
[0.8\|z\|_{T},1.2\|z\|_{T}]
because of Property II.
∎

We assume
z_{\emp}\in[0.8\|z\|_{T},1.2\|z\|_{T}]
and focus on
U=\{t\in[0,T]\big||z(t)|\geq 0.5z_{\emp}\}
. Notice that

\int_{U}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{T}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t-\int_{[0,T]\setminus U}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\geq(1-0.6^{2})\int_{0}^{T}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

Let
R_{\heavy}=|S_{\heavy}|
. From Claim
7.8
and
\epsilon
,
\E[R_{\heavy}]\geq R_{\repeats}/(T\Delta)
. So we assume
R_{\heavy}\geq 0.01R_{\repeats}/(T\Delta)=0.01(T\Delta)^{2}
in the rest of this section and think each
\alpha_{i}\in S_{\heavy}
is a uniform sample from
U
over the randomness on
S_{\heavy}
.

Claim 7.12
.

With probability 0.95,
\sum_{i\in S_{\heavy}}(|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i})|^{2}+|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|^{2})\leq 10^{-4}\sum_{i\in S_{\heavy}}(|z(\alpha_{i})|^{2}+|z(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|^{2})
for a sufficiently small
\epsilon
and
\|z^{\overline{I}}\|^{2}_{T}\leq\epsilon\|z\|^{2}_{T}
.

Proof.

At first,

\E_{S_{\heavy}}\left[\sum_{i\in S_{\heavy}}(|z(\alpha_{i})|^{2}+|z(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|^{2})\right]\geq R_{\heavy}\cdot\E_{t\sim U}[|z(t)|^{2}]=R_{\heavy}\cdot\frac{\int_{U}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t}{|U|}.

At the same time,

\E_{S_{\heavy}}\left[\sum_{i\in S_{\heavy}}[|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i})|^{2}+|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|^{2}]\right]=R_{\heavy}\cdot\E_{t\sim U}[|z^{\overline{I}}(t)|^{2}+|z^{\overline{I}}(t+\beta)|^{2}]\leq\frac{2\int^{0}_{T}|z^{\overline{I}}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t}{|U|}.

From
\int_{U}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\geq 0.64\int_{0}^{T}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t
and
\int^{0}_{T}|z^{\overline{I}}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\leq\epsilon\int_{0}^{T}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t
, we get the conclusion.
∎

We assume all results in the above claims hold and prove that the sample from
S_{\heavy}
is a good sample such that
z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha)
is small.

Claim 7.13
.

If we sample
i\in S_{\heavy}
according to the weight
|z(\alpha_{i})|^{2}+|z(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|^{2}
, with prob. at least
0.9
,
|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i})|+|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|\leq 0.05(|z(\alpha_{i})|+|z(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|)
.

Proof.

Similar to the proof of the key observation, we compute the expectation of
\frac{|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i})|^{2}+|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|^{2}}{|z(\alpha_{i})|^{2}+|z(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|^{2}}
over the sampling in
S_{\heavy}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S_{\heavy}}\frac{|z(\alpha_{i})|^{2}+|z(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|^{2}}{\underset{j\in S_{\heavy}}{\sum}|z(\alpha_{j})|^{2}+|z(\alpha_{j}+\beta)|^{2}}\cdot\frac{|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i})|^{2}+|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|^{2}}{|z(\alpha_{i})|^{2}+|z(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\underset{i\in S_{\heavy}}{\sum}|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i})|^{2}+|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|^{2}}{\underset{i\in S_{\heavy}}{\sum}|z(\alpha_{i})|^{2}+|z(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 10^{-4}.

By Markov’s inequality, when we sample
i\in S_{\heavy}
according to the weight
|z(\alpha_{i})|^{2}+|z(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|^{2}
,
\frac{|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i})|^{2}+|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|^{2}}{|z(\alpha_{i})|^{2}+|z(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|^{2}}\leq 10^{-3}
with probability
0.9
. We have that with prob. at least
0.9
,
|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i})|+|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|\leq 0.05(|z(\alpha_{i})|+|z(\alpha_{i}+\beta)|)
.
∎

We assume all above claims hold and finish the proof by setting
\alpha=\alpha_{i}
. From Claim
7.9
, we know that

|z^{I}(\alpha)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}-z^{I}(\alpha+\beta)|\leq 0.01\cdot\E_{t\in[0,T]}[|z^{I}(\alpha)|^{2}]^{1/2}\leq 0.03|z^{I}(\alpha)|.

Now we add back the noise
z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha)
and
z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha+\beta)
to get

|z(\alpha)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}-z(\alpha+\beta)|\leq|z^{I}(\alpha)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}-z^{I}(\alpha+\beta)|+|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha)|+|z^{\overline{I}}(\alpha+\beta)|\leq 0.08(|z(\alpha)|+|z(\alpha+\beta)|).

7.3
A cluster of frequencies, times
H
, is a one-cluster signal per Definition
7.1

The goal of this section is to prove Lemma
7.4
.
Without loss of generality, we assume
g(t)=0
for any
t\notin[0,T]
and notice that
\supp(\widehat{H}*\widehat{x}^{*})\subseteq f_{0}+[-\Delta,\Delta]
for
\Delta=\Delta^{\prime}+\Delta_{h}
from the definition of
\widehat{H}
. From the Property VI (presented in Lemma
6.6
) of
(H,\widehat{H})
,

\int_{0}^{T}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t=(1\pm c)\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|H(t)\cdot x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

From the first two properties of
(H,\widehat{H})
, we bound the energy of
g\cdot H
:

\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|H(t)\cdot g(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\leq(1+c)\int_{0}^{T}|g(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

Let
z(t)=(x^{*}(t)+g(t))H(t)
. We use the triangle inequality on the above two inequalities:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \int_{0}^{T}|H(t)\cdot x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{T}|H(t)\cdot g(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t-2\int_{0}^{T}|H(t)\cdot x^{*}(t)|\cdot|H(t)\cdot g(t)|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1-c)\int_{0}^{T}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t-(1+c)\int_{0}^{T}|g(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t-2\sqrt{(1+c)^{2}\int_{0}^{T}|g(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\int_{0}^{T}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\cdot}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \left(1-5\sqrt{c}\right)\int_{0}^{T}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

where we use the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and
\int_{0}^{T}|g(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\leq c\int_{0}^{T}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t
in the last step. Similarly,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1+c)\int_{0}^{T}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t+(1+c)\int_{0}^{T}|g(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t+2\sqrt{(1+c)^{2}\int_{0}^{T}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\int_{0}^{T}|g(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1+5\sqrt{c})\int_{0}^{T}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

Hence we obtain Property II(in Definition
7.1
) when
c
is sufficiently small.

Then we observe that

\displaystyle\penalty\ \int_{f_{0}-\Delta_{h}}^{f_{0}+\Delta_{h}}|\widehat{z}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \int_{f_{0}-\Delta_{h}}^{f_{0}+\Delta_{h}}|\widehat{H\cdot(x^{*}+g)}|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \int_{f_{0}-\Delta_{h}}^{f_{0}+\Delta_{h}}|\widehat{H\cdot x^{*}}|^{2}-|\widehat{H\cdot g}|^{2}-2|\widehat{H\cdot x^{*}}|\cdot|\widehat{H\cdot g}|\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \int_{f_{0}-\Delta_{h}}^{f_{0}+\Delta_{h}}|\widehat{H\cdot x^{*}}|^{2}\mathrm{d}f-\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|\widehat{H\cdot g}|^{2}\mathrm{d}f-2\sqrt{\int_{f_{0}-\Delta_{h}}^{f_{0}+\Delta_{h}}|\widehat{H\cdot x^{*}}|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|\widehat{H\cdot g}|^{2}\mathrm{d}f}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|H\cdot x^{*}|^{2}\mathrm{d}t-\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|H\cdot g|^{2}\mathrm{d}t-2\sqrt{\int_{f_{0}-\Delta_{h}}^{f_{0}+\Delta_{h}}|H\cdot x^{*}|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|H\cdot g|^{2}\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{(1-c)-c(1+c)-3\sqrt{c}}{1+5\sqrt{c}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

Thus we have Property I(in Definition
7.1
) for
z
.

7.4
Frequency recovery of one-cluster signals

The goal of this section is prove Theorem
7.5
. We first show the correctness of Procedure
Locate1Inner
. Second, we analyze the Procedure
Locate1Signal
. At end, we rerun Procedure
Locate1Signal
and use median analysis to boost the constant success probability.
3
3

3

The proofs in this section are identical to
[
HIKP12b
]
and
[
PS15
]
.

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
f_{0}\in\mathrm{region}(q^{\prime})
. Let
\beta
is sampled from
[\frac{st}{4\Delta},\frac{st}{2\Delta l}]
and let
\gamma
denote the output of Procedure
GetLegal1Sample
in Algorithm
4
. Then using the pair of samples
z(\gamma+\beta)
and
z(\gamma)
, we have

I. for the
q^{\prime}
with probability at least
1-s
,
v_{q^{\prime}}
will increase by one.

II. for any
q
such that
|q-q^{\prime}|>3
, with probability at least
1-15s
,
v_{q}
will not increase.

Proof.

We replace
f_{0}
by
\theta
in the rest of the proof.
By Lemma
7.2
, we have that for any
\widehat{\beta}\leq\beta\leq 2\widehat{\beta}
, Procedure
GetLegal1Sample
outputs a
\gamma\in[0,T]
satisfying

|z(\gamma+\beta)-z(\gamma)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|\leq 0.1(|z(\gamma)|+|z(\gamma+\beta)|)

with probability at least 0.6.

Furthermore, there exists such some constant
g\in(0,1)
such that with probability
1-g
,

\|\phi(z(\gamma+\beta))-(\phi(z(\gamma))-2\pi\beta\theta)\|_{\bigcirc}\lesssim\sin^{-1}(\frac{1}{g}),

where
\|x-y\|_{\bigcirc}=\underset{z\in\mathbb{Z}}{\min}|x-y+2\pi z|
denote the “circular distance” between
x
and
y
.
We can set
s=\Theta(g^{-1})
.
There exists some constant
p=\Theta(s)
, with probability at least

1-p
,

\|o-2\pi\beta\theta\|_{\bigcirc}<s\pi/2

where
o:=\phi(z(\gamma+\beta)/z(\gamma))
. The above equation shows that
o
is a good estimate for
2\pi\beta\theta
with good probability. We will now show that this means the true region
Q_{q^{\prime}}
gets a vote with large probability.

For each
q^{\prime}
with
\theta\in[l-\frac{\Delta l}{2}+\frac{q^{\prime}-1}{t}\Delta l,l-\frac{\Delta l}{2}+\frac{q^{\prime}}{t}\Delta l]\subset[-F,F]
, we have that
\theta_{q^{\prime}}=l-\frac{\Delta l}{2}+\frac{q^{\prime}-0.5}{t}\Delta l
satisfies that

\theta-\theta_{q^{\prime}}\leq\frac{\Delta l}{2t}.

Note that we sample
\beta
uniformly at random from
[\widehat{\beta},2\widehat{\beta}]
, then
2\widehat{\beta}=\frac{st}{2\Delta l}\leq\frac{cT}{10A^{\frac{3}{2}}}
(Note that
A
is some constant
>1
), which implies that
2\pi\beta\frac{\Delta l}{2t}\leq\frac{s\pi}{2}
. Thus, we can show the observation
o
is close to the true region in the following sense,

\displaystyle\|o-2\pi\beta\theta_{q^{\prime}}\|_{\bigcirc}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|o-2\pi\beta\theta\|_{\bigcirc}+\|2\pi\beta\theta-2\pi\beta\theta_{q^{\prime}}\|_{\bigcirc}\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ triangle\penalty\ inequality}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{s\pi}{2}+2\pi\|\beta\theta-\beta\theta_{q^{\prime}}\|_{\bigcirc}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle s\pi.

Thus,
v_{q^{\prime}}
will increase in each round with probability at least
1-s
.

On the other side, consider
q
with
|q-q^{\prime}|>3
. Then
|\theta-\theta_{q}|\geq\frac{7\Delta l}{2t}
, and (assuming
\beta\geq\frac{st}{4\Delta l}
) we have

2\pi\beta|\theta-\theta_{q}|\geq 2\pi\frac{st}{4\Delta l}|\theta-\theta_{q}|=\frac{s\pi t}{2\Delta l}|\theta-\theta_{q}|\geq\frac{7s\pi}{4}>\frac{3s\pi}{2}.

There are two cases:
|\theta-\theta_{q}|\leq\frac{\Delta l}{st}
and
|\theta-\theta_{q}|>\frac{\Delta l}{st}
.

First, if
|\theta-\theta_{q}|\leq\frac{\Delta l}{st}
. In this case, from the definition of
\beta
it follows that

2\pi\beta|\theta-\theta_{q}|\leq\frac{s\pi t}{\Delta l}|\theta-\theta_{q}|\leq\pi

Combining the above equations implies that

\mathsf{Pr}\bigl[2\pi\beta(\theta-\theta_{q})\pmod{2\pi}\in[-\frac{3s}{4}2\pi,\frac{3s}{4}2\pi]\bigr]=0

Second, if
|\theta-\theta_{q}|>\frac{\Delta l}{st}
. We show this claim is true :

\mathsf{Pr}[2\pi\beta(\theta-\theta_{q})\pmod{2\pi}\in[-\frac{3s}{4}2\pi,\frac{3s}{4}2\pi]]\lesssim s
. To prove it, we apply Lemma
6.5
by setting
\widetilde{T}=2\pi
,
\widetilde{\sigma}=2\pi\beta
,
\widetilde{\delta}=0
,
\epsilon=\frac{3s}{4}2\pi
,
A=2\pi\widehat{\beta}
,
\Delta f=|\theta-\theta_{q}|
. By upper bound of Lemma
6.5
, the probability is at most

\frac{2\widetilde{\epsilon}}{\widetilde{T}}+\frac{4\widetilde{\epsilon}}{A\Delta f}=\frac{3s}{2}+\frac{3s}{\widehat{\beta}\Delta f}\leq\frac{3s}{2}+\frac{3s}{\frac{st}{4\Delta l}\frac{\Delta l}{st}}<15s

Then in either case, with probability at least
1-15s
, we have

\|2\pi\beta\theta_{q}-2\pi\beta\theta\|_{\bigcirc}>\frac{3s}{4}2\pi

which implies that
v_{q}
will not increase.
∎

Lemma 7.15
.

Procedure
Locate1Inner
in Algorithm
4
uses
R_{\loc}
“legal” samples, and then after Procedure
Locate1Signal
in Algorithm
4
running Procedure
Locate1Inner

D_{\max}
times, it outputs a frequency
\widetilde{f}_{0}
such that

|\widetilde{f}_{0}-f_{0}|\lesssim\Delta\cdot\sqrt{T\Delta}

with arbitrarily large constant probability.

Proof.

For each observation,
v_{q^{\prime}}
incremented with probability at least
1-p
and
v_{q}
is incremented with probability at most
15s+p
for
|q-q^{\prime}|>3
. The probabilities corresponding to different observations are independent. Then after
R_{\loc}
observations, there exists some constant
c<\frac{1}{2}
, for any
q
such that
|q-q^{\prime}|>3
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}[\text{False\penalty\ region\penalty\ gets\penalty\ more\penalty\ than\penalty\ half\penalty\ votes}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}[v_{j,q}>R_{\loc}/2]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\binom{R_{\loc}}{R_{\loc}/2}(15s+p)^{R_{\loc}/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c^{\Omega(R_{\loc})}

Similarly, on the other side,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}[\text{True\penalty\ region\penalty\ gets\penalty\ less\penalty\ than\penalty\ half\penalty\ votes}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}[v_{j,q^{\prime}}<R_{\loc}/2]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\binom{R_{\loc}}{R_{\loc}/2}(p)^{R_{\loc}/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c^{\Omega(R_{\loc})}

Taking the union bound over all the
t
regions, it gives with probability at least
1-tf^{\Omega(R_{\loc})}
we can find some region
q
such that
|q-q^{\prime}|<3
.

If we repeat the above procedure
D_{\max}
rounds, each round we choose the “False” region with probability at most
1-tc^{\Omega(R_{\loc})}
. Thus, taking the union bound over all the
D_{\max}
rounds, we will report a region has size
\eqsim\Delta\sqrt{\Delta T}
and contains
f_{0}
with probability at least
1-D_{\max}tc^{\Omega(R_{\loc})}
.

The reason for not ending up with region that has size
\eqsim\Delta
is, the upper bound of the sample range of
\beta
force us to choose
\beta
is at most
\lesssim\frac{T}{(\Delta T)^{\frac{3}{2}}}
by Claim
7.9

It remains to explain how to set
D_{\max},t,
and
R_{\loc}
. At the beginning of the first round, we start with frequency interval of length
2F
, at the beginning of the last round, we start with frequency interval of length
t\cdot\Delta\sqrt{T\Delta}
. Each round we do a
t
-ary search, thus

D_{\max}=\log_{t}(\frac{2F}{t\Delta\sqrt{T\Delta}})\leq\log_{t}(F/\Delta).

We can set
R_{\loc}\eqsim\log_{1/c}(t/c)
and
t>D_{\max}
, e.g.
t=\log(F/\Delta)
. Thus, the probability becomes,

1-D_{\max}tc^{\Omega(R_{\loc})}\geq 1-t^{2}c^{\Omega(R_{\loc})}\geq 1-\poly(1/t,c)

which is larger than any constant probability.
∎

Using the same parameters setting in the proof of Lemma
7.15
, we show the running time and sample complexity of Procedure
Locate1Signal
,

Lemma 7.16
.

Procedure
Locate1Signal
in Algorithm
4
uses

O(\poly(k,\log(1/\delta)))\cdot\log(FT)
samples and runs in
O(\poly(k,\log(1/\delta)))\cdot\log^{2}(FT)
time.

Proof.

The number of “legal” observations is

D_{\max}R_{\loc}=O(\log_{t}(F/\Delta)\log_{1/c}(t/c))=O(\log(F/\Delta))

The total number of samples is

R_{\est}+R_{\repeats}D_{\max}R_{\loc}=O(T\Delta_{h})^{2}+(T\Delta_{h})^{3}\cdot\log(FT)=\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\cdot\log(FT)

where the first step follows by Claim
7.11
and Lemma
7.2
and the last step follows by the setting of
\Delta_{h}
in Appendix
C.3
.

The running time includes two parts, one is approximately computing
H(t)
for all the samples, each sample takes
\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))
time according to Lemma
C.8
; the other is for each legal sample we need to assign vote to some regions.

\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\cdot(R_{\est}+R_{\repeats}D_{\max}R_{\loc})+D_{\max}R_{\loc}t=\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\log^{2}(FT)

∎

Lemma
7.17
only achieves constant success probability, using median analysis we can boost the success probability,

Lemma 7.17
.

Let
\widetilde{f_{0}}
denote the frequency output by Procedure
FrequencyRecovery1Cluster
in Algorithm
5
, then with probability at least
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
,

|\widetilde{f}_{0}-f_{0}|\lesssim\Delta\sqrt{T\Delta}

Proof.

Because of Procedure
FrequencyRecovery1Cluster
taking the median of
O(k)
independent results by repeating algorithm
Locate1Signal

O(k)
times. Each sample
L_{r}
is close to
\widetilde{f}_{0}
with sufficiently large probability. Thus, using the Chernoff bound will output
\widetilde{f}_{0}
with probability
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
such that

|\widetilde{f}_{0}-f_{0}|\lesssim\Delta\sqrt{T\Delta}.

∎

Combining Lemma
7.17
with the sample complexity and running time in Lemma
7.15
, we are able to finish the proof of Theorem
7.5
.

7.5
The full signal, after multiplying by
H
and convolving with
G
, is one-clustered.

The goal of this section is to prove Lemma
7.6
.
We fix
f^{*}\in[-F,F]
satisfying (
17
) in this section. We first define a good hashing
(\sigma,b)
of
f^{*}
as follows.

Definition 7.18
.

We say that a frequency
f^{*}
is
well-isolated
under the
hashing
(\sigma,b)
if, for
j=h_{\sigma,b}(f^{*})
, we have
that the signal

\widehat{z}^{(j)}=\widehat{x\cdot H}\cdot\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}

satisfies, over the interval
\overline{I_{f^{*}}}=(-\infty,\infty)\setminus(f^{*}-\Delta,f^{*}+\Delta)
,

\int_{\overline{I_{f^{*}}}}|\widehat{z}^{(j)}(f)|^{2}df\lesssim\epsilon\cdot T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k.

For convenience, we simplify
z^{(j)}
by using
z
in the rest of this section.

Lemma 7.19
.

Let
f^{*}
be any frequency. Then
f^{*}
is well-isolated by a
hashing
(\sigma,b)
with probability
\geq 0.9
given
B=\Theta(k)
and
\sigma\in[\frac{1}{B\Delta},\frac{2}{B\Delta}]
chosen uniformly at random.

Proof.

For any other frequency
f^{\prime}
in
x^{*}
, its contribution in
\widehat{z}
depends on how far
it is from
f^{*}
. Either it is:

•

Within
\Delta
of
f^{*}
,
f^{\prime}
and
f^{*}
will be mapped into the same bucket with probability at least
0.99
.

•

Between
\Delta
and
1/\sigma
far, from Claim
6.4
,
f^{\prime}
and
f^{*}
will always mapped into different buckets. Hence
f^{\prime}
always
contributes in the
\frac{\epsilon\delta}{k}
region of Property III in Lemma
6.7
about filter function
(G(t),\widehat{G}(f))
, i.e., it contributes at most
\frac{\epsilon\delta}{k}\cdot\int_{f^{\prime}-\Delta}^{f^{\prime}+\Delta}|\widehat{x\cdot H}|^{2}\mathrm{d}f
. Overall it will contribute

\frac{\epsilon\delta}{k}\cdot\int|\widehat{x\cdot H}|^{2}\mathrm{d}f=\frac{\epsilon\delta}{k}\int|x\cdot H|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

•

More than
1/\sigma
far, in which case they contribute in the
same region with probability at most
3/B
. By a union bound, it is at most
3k/B\leq 0.01

∎

Without loss of generality, we assume
\supp(\widehat{g\cdot H})\cap\supp(\widehat{x^{*}\cdot H})=\emptyset
, otherwise we treat it as a part of
x^{*}\cdot H
. We first consider frequency
f^{*}\in\widehat{x^{*}\cdot H}
under
G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}
.

Lemma 7.20
.

Let
f^{*}
satisfying
\int_{f^{*}-\Delta}^{f^{*}+\Delta}|\widehat{x^{*}\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k

and
\widehat{z}=\widehat{x^{*}\cdot H}\cdot\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}
where
j=h_{\sigma,b}(f^{*})
. If
f^{*}
is well-isolated, then
z
and
\widehat{z}
satisfying Property I(in Definition
7.1
), i.e.,

\int_{0}^{T}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\geq(1-\epsilon)\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

Proof.

We first notice that
z(t)=x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t)*G_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(t)
and lower bound
\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t
as follows :

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t)*G_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|\widehat{x^{*}\cdot H}(f)\cdot\widehat{G}_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

by FT

\displaystyle\geq\quad

\displaystyle\int_{f_{0}-\Delta}^{f_{0}+\Delta}|\widehat{x^{*}\cdot H}(f)\cdot\widehat{G}_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\geq\quad

\displaystyle(1-\delta)^{2}\int_{f_{0}-\Delta}^{f_{0}+\Delta}|\widehat{x^{*}\cdot H}(f)\ |^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\geq\quad

\displaystyle(1-\delta)^{2}T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k

\displaystyle\geq\quad

\displaystyle 0.9\frac{\delta}{k}\int_{0}^{T}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

(19)

We give an upper bound
\int_{-\infty}^{0}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t+\int_{T}^{+\infty}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\epsilon\frac{\delta}{k}\int_{0}^{T}|x^{*}(t)H(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t
in the rest of this proof.

Consider the case
t<0
, by definition of Convolution,

\displaystyle z^{(j)}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t)*G_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}G_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(t-\tau)\cdot(x^{*}\cdot H)(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau

Without loss of generality, we can shift the original signal and
H(t)
from
[0,T]
to
[-T/2,T/2]
, by Property of
H(t)
, we know that if
s_{3}T/2\leq|t|\leq T/2
, then
H(t)\leq 2^{-O\Theta(\ell)}
. Note that
G(t)
is compact and has support
DB
, we also assume its compact region is
[-DB/2,DB/2]
(Recall that
D=\frac{l}{\alpha\pi}
).

Thus, by definition of convolution,

\displaystyle\penalty\ z(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \int_{-DB\sigma/2}^{DB\sigma/2}{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(s)\cdot(x\cdot H)(t-\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1}{\sigma}\int_{-DB\sigma/2}^{DB\sigma/2}{G}(s/\sigma)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}s(j/B-\sigma b)/\sigma}\cdot(x\cdot H)(t-\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1}{\sigma}\int_{-DB\sigma/2}^{DB\sigma/2}|{G}(\tau/\sigma)|\cdot|(x\cdot H)(t-\tau)|\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \left(\frac{1}{\sigma}\int_{-DB\sigma/2}^{DB\sigma/2}|{G}(\tau/\sigma)|\mathrm{d}\tau\right)\cdot\left(\underset{|\tau|\leq DB\sigma/2}{\max}|(x\cdot H)(t-\tau)|\right)

So, if
t\notin[-T/2,T/2]
, then
t-s\notin[-T/2+DB\sigma/2,T/2-DB\sigma/2]
. By Property V of
G(t)
,
|G(t)|\leq\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))
. Because of the parameter setting
4
4

4

We will set
B
to be
O(k)
,
D
to be
\poly(k)
and
\sigma
to be
T/\poly(k)
.
, we have the fact
[-Ts_{3}/2,Ts_{3}/2]\subseteq[-T/2+DB\sigma/2,T/2-DB\sigma/2]\subseteq[-T/2,T/2]
. Thus, we know
T(1-s_{3})/2>DB\sigma/2
, then for any
t-\tau\in[-T/2,-T/2+DB\sigma/2]\cup[T/2-DB\sigma/2,T/2]=S
, then

|z(t)|^{2}\lesssim\bigl(DB\sigma\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma}\cdot\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\bigr)^{2}\cdot 2^{-\Theta(\ell)}\cdot k^{4}\cdot\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\cdot 2^{-\Theta(\ell)}\cdot\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

Thus, taking the integral over
S
,

\int_{S}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\lesssim|S|\cdot 2^{-\Theta(\ell)}\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\cdot\|x^{*}(t)\|^{2}\lesssim 2^{-\Theta(\ell)}T\|x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t)\|_{T}^{2}

By property of filter function
H(t),\widehat{H}(f)
, we have

|(x\cdot H)(t)|^{2}\leq(\frac{t}{T})^{-\ell}\|x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t)\|_{T}^{2}\text{\penalty\ if\penalty\ }t\geq 3T

Thus for any constant
\epsilon
,

\int_{-\infty}^{-T/2}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t+\int_{T/2}^{+\infty}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\lesssim 2^{-\ell}T\|x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq 0.9\epsilon\cdot\frac{\delta}{k}\int_{-T/2}^{T/2}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

(20)

where the last inequality follows by
\ell\gtrsim k\log(k/\delta)
.
Shifting the interval from
[-T/2,T/2]
to
[0,T]
, the same result is still holding. Combining Equation (
7.5
) and (
20
) completes the proof of Property II.

∎

We consider frequency
f^{*}\in\widehat{g\cdot H}
under
G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}
and show the energy of noise
g(t)
is evenly distributed over
B
bins on expectation.

Lemma 7.21
.

Given any noise
g(t):[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
and
g(t)=0,\forall t\notin[0,T]
. We have,
\forall j\in[B],

\underset{\sigma,b}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|g(t)H(t)*G_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\right]\lesssim\frac{1}{B}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|g(t)H(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

Proof.

Because of Fourier Transform preserves
\ell_{2}
norm, it suffices to prove

\underset{\sigma,b}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|\widehat{g\cdot H}(f)\cdot\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\right]\lesssim\frac{1}{B}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|\widehat{g\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

Since
\widehat{G}_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(f)
is a periodic function and outputs at most
1
on
O(1/B)
fraction of the period, and outputs
\leq\delta
on other part. Thus, for any frequency
f
, we have

\underset{\sigma,b}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|\widehat{G}_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(f)|^{2}\right]\lesssim\frac{1}{B}

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\underset{\sigma,b}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|\widehat{g\cdot H}(f)\cdot\widehat{G}_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\underset{\sigma,b}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|\widehat{g\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\cdot|\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|\widehat{g\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\cdot\underset{\sigma,b}{\mathbb{E}}[|\widehat{G}_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(f)|^{2}]\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|\widehat{g\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\cdot\underset{f}{\max}\left[\underset{\sigma,b}{\mathbb{E}}\left|\widehat{G}_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(f)\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{B}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|\widehat{g\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f,

which completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
7.6
.

Let
j=h_{\sigma,b}(f^{*})
, signal

\widehat{z}=\widehat{x\cdot H}\cdot\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b},

(21)

and region
I_{f^{*}}=(f^{*}-\Delta,f^{*}+\Delta)
with complement

\overline{I_{f^{*}}}=(-\infty,\infty)\setminus I_{f^{*}}
.
From Property I of
G
in Lemma
6.7
, we have that

\widehat{G}^{(l)}_{\sigma,b}(f)\gtrsim 1

for all
f\in I_{f^{*}}
, so by (
17
)

\int_{I_{f^{*}}}|\widehat{z}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k.

On the other hand,
f^{*}
is will-isolated with probability
0.9
:

\int_{\overline{I_{f^{*}}}}|\widehat{z}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\lesssim\epsilon T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k.

Hence,
\widehat{z}
satisfies the
Property I(in Definition
7.1
) of one-mountain recovery. Combining Lemma
7.20
and Lemma
7.21
, we know that
(x^{*}\cdot H)*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}
always satisfies Property II(in Definition
7.1
) and
\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|g(t)H(t)*G_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t
is less than
20T\mathcal{N}^{2}/B\leq\epsilon T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k
with probability at least
0.95
, which indicates that
z=(x^{*}+g)\cdot H*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}
satisfies Property II(in Definition
7.1
) with probability
0.95
. ∎

7.6
Frequency recovery of
\texorpdfstring
k
k-clustered signals

The goal of this section is to prove that the frequencies found by Procedure
FrequencyRecoveryKCluster
in Algorithm
8
have some reasonable guarantee.

We first notice that Lemma
7.6
and Lemma
7.3
imply the following
lemma by a union bound.

Lemma 7.22
.

Let
x^{*}(t)=\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
. We observe
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
, where
\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq c\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}
for a sufficiently small constant
c
and define
\mathcal{N}^{2}:=\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}+\delta\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}
. Then
Procedure
OneStage

returns a set
L
of
O(k)

frequencies that covers the heavy frequencies of
x^{*}
. In
particular, for any
f^{*}
with

\int_{f^{*}-\Delta}^{f^{*}+\Delta}|\widehat{x\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k,

(22)

there will exist an
\widetilde{f}\in L
satisfying
|f^{*}-\widetilde{f}|\lesssim\sqrt{T\Delta}\cdot\Delta T
with probability
0.99
.

Lemma 7.23
.

Let
x^{*}(t)=\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
and
R=O(k)
. We observe
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
, where
\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq c\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}
for a sufficiently small constant
c
and choose
\mathcal{N}^{2}:=\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}+\delta\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}
. Then
Algorithm
MultipleStages

returns a set
L
of
O(k)

frequencies that approximates the heavy frequencies of
x^{*}
. In
particular, with probability
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
, for any
f^{*}
such that

\int_{f^{*}-\Delta}^{f^{*}+\Delta}|\widehat{x\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k,

(23)

there will exist an
\widetilde{f}\in L
satisfying
|f^{*}-\widetilde{f}|\lesssim\sqrt{T\Delta}\Delta
.

Proof.

Let
A\subset[-F,F]
denote the set of frequencies
f^{*}

satisfying Equation (
22
). Let
A^{\prime}\subset[-F,F]
denote a
net of
A
of distance
2\Delta
, so the intervals used
in Equation (
22
) for each
f^{*}\in A^{\prime}
are disjoint. Then

|A^{\prime}|\leq 2k+k=3k

because each frequency in
x^{*}
contributes to at most two of the
intervals, and the total mass of
\widehat{g}
is at most
k
times the
threshold
T\mathcal{N}^{2}
.

Let
L_{1},\dotsc,L_{R}
be the results of
R
rounds of
Algorithm
OneStage
.
We say that a frequency
f\in A^{\prime}
is
successfully recovered in round
r
if there
exists an
\widetilde{f}\in L_{r}
such that
|f-\widetilde{f}|\leq\Delta_{a}
, where

\Delta_{a}=\Delta\sqrt{T\Delta}\lesssim\sqrt{T\Delta}\Delta.

By
Lemma
7.22
, each frequency is successfully
recovered with
0.8
probability in each round. Then by the Chernoff
bound, with
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
probability, every
f\in A^{\prime}
will be
successfully recovered in at least
0.6R
rounds.

Then, by Lemma
7.24
, we output a set
L
of
O(B)

frequencies such that every
f\in A^{\prime}
is within
\Delta_{a}
of some

\widetilde{f}\in L
. Hence every
f\in A
is within
2\Delta_{a}
of some

\widetilde{f}\in L
.
∎

Lemma 7.24
.

Let
L_{1},\dotsc,L_{R}
by sets of frequencies and
f^{*}
be any
frequency. Then
L=\textsc{MergedStages}(L_{1},

\dotsc,

L_{R})
is a set of

2\frac{\sum|L_{r}|}{R}
frequencies satisfying

\min_{\widetilde{f}\in L}|f^{*}-\widetilde{f}|\leq\median_{r\in[R]}\min_{f\in L_{r}}|f^{*}-f|.

Proof.

The algorithm is to take the union, sort, and take every

\frac{R}{2}
th entry of the sorted list.

Let
\Delta=\median_{r\in[R]}\min_{f\in L_{r}}|f^{*}-f|
.
We have that at least
R/2
different
f\in\bigcup_{r}L_{r}
lie
within
\Delta
of
f^{*}
. This set forms a sequential subsequence
of the sorted list of frequencies, so our output will include one.
∎

7.7
Time and sample complexity of frequency recovery of
k
-clustered signals

The goal of this section is to show that Procedure
FrequencyRecoveryKCluster
takes

\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\log(FT)
samples, and runs in
\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\log^{2}(FT)
time.

In order to analyze the running time and sample complexity. We need to extend the one-cluster version Procedure
GetLegal1Sample
and
GetEmpirical1Energy
(in Algorithm
3
) to
k
-cluster version
GetLegalKSample
and
GetEmpiricalKEnergy
(in Algorithm
7
)
5

5

We omitted the proofs here, because the proofs are identical to the one-cluster situation.
,

Lemma 7.25
.

Procedure

runs Procedure

R_{\repeats}=O((T\Delta)^{3})

\widehat{v},\widehat{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{B}
such that, for each

|\widehat{v}_{j}-\widehat{v}^{\prime}_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}\beta}|\leq 0.08(|\widehat{v}_{j}|+|\widehat{v}^{\prime}_{j}|),

z

.

Claim 7.27
.

Algorithm
LocateKSignal
in Algorithm
6
uses
O(\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\cdot\log(FT))
, and runs in
O(\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\cdot\log^{2}(FT))
.

Proof.

We first calculate the number of samples. All the samples is basically all the Fourier samples, each time needs
B\log(k/\delta)
. In total it calls
HashToBins

O(R_{\est}+R_{\repeats}D_{\max}R_{\loc})
times where
D_{\max}R_{\loc}=\Theta(\log(FT))
by similar analysis as one-cluster frequency recovery. Thus, the total number of samples is

\displaystyle(R_{\est}+R_{\repeats}D_{\max}R_{\loc})B\log(k/\delta)=\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\cdot\log(FT).

Then, we analyze the running time.

The expected running time includes the following parts: the first part is running Procedure
HashToBins

O(R_{\est}+R_{\repeats}D_{\max}R_{loc})
times, each run takes
O(B\log(k/\delta)+B\log B)
samples. For each such sample we need
\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))
time to compute
H(t)
according to Lemma
C.8
and there are
\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\log(FT))
many samples; the second part is updating the counter
v
,which takes
O(D_{\max}R_{loc}Bt)
time. Thus, in total

\displaystyle\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\cdot O(R_{\est}+R_{\repeats}D_{\max}R_{\loc})\cdot O(B\log(k/\delta)+B\log B)+O(D_{\max}R_{loc}Bt)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\cdot\log^{2}(FT),

where by similar analysis as one-cluster recovery,
t=\Theta(\log(FT))
and
D_{\max}R_{\loc}=\Theta(\log(FT))
.
∎

To boost the success probability, Procedure

times. At the end, Procedure

.

uses

, and runs in

.

8
One-cluster Signal Recovery

8.1
Overview

In this section, we consider
x^{*}
whose frequencies in
\widehat{x}^{*}
are in the range
[f_{0}-\Delta^{\prime},f_{0}+\Delta^{\prime}]
for some frequency
f_{0}
and
\Delta^{\prime}>0
and provide an algorithm to approximate it by a polynomial.

We fix
T
in this section and recall that
\langle f(t),g(t)\rangle_{T}:=\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}f(t)\overline{g}(t)\mathrm{d}t
such that
\|e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{i}t}\|_{T}=\sqrt{\langle e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{i}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{i}t}\rangle_{T}}=1
. For convenience, given
\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
, we say the frequency gap of this signal is
\underset{i\neq j}{\min}|f_{i}-f_{j}|
.

For simplicity, we first consider frequencies clustered around 0. The main technical lemma in this section is that any signal
x^{*}
with bounded frequencies in
\widehat{x}^{*}
can be approximated by a low-degree polynomial on
[0,T]
.

Lemma
2.3
.

For any
\Delta>0
and any
\delta>0
, let
x^{*}(t)=\sum_{j\in[k]}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
where
|f_{j}|\leq\Delta
for each
j\in[k]
. There exists a polynomial
P(t)
of degree at most

d=O(T\Delta+k^{3}\log k+k\log 1/\delta)

such that

\|P(t)-x^{*}(t)\|^{2}_{T}\leq\delta\|x^{*}\|^{2}_{T}.

One direct corollary is that when
\widehat{x}^{*}
are in the range
[f_{0}+\Delta^{\prime},f_{0}+\Delta^{\prime}]
, we can approximate
x^{*}
by
P(t)\cdot e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}t}
for some low degree polynomial
P
.

We give an overview of this section first.
We first show some technical tools in Section
8.2
,
8.3
. In Section
8.4
, using those tools, we can show for any
k
-Fourier-sparse signal, there exists another
k
-Fourier-sparse signal with bounded frequency gap close to the original signal. In Section
8.5
, we show that for any
k
-Fourier-sparse signal with bounded frequency gap, then there exists a low degree polynomial close to it. In Section
8.6
, we show how to transfer low degree polynomial back to a Fourier-sparse signal. Combining all the above steps finishes the proof of Lemma
2.3
.

We apply Theorem
7.5
of frequency estimation on
x^{*}
to obtain an estimation
\widetilde{f_{0}}
of
f_{0}
and use Theorem
4.5
on the approximation
Q(t)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f_{0}}t}
of
x^{*}
to recover the signal. We summarize this result as follows.

Theorem 8.1

(One-cluster Signal Recovery)
.

Let
x^{*}(t)=\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
where
\forall j\in[k],|f_{j}-f_{0}|\leq\Delta
and
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
be our observable signal. For any
\delta>0
and any
T>0
, let
\mathcal{N}^{2}:=\|g\|^{2}_{T}+\delta\|x^{*}\|_{T}^{2}
. Procedure
CFT1Culster
in Algorithm
5
finds a polynomial
P(t)
of degree at most
d=O\left((T\Delta_{h}+T\Delta)^{1.5}+k^{3}\log k+k\log 1/\delta\right)
and a frequency
\widetilde{f}_{0}
such that

\|P(t)\cdot e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{0}t}-x^{*}(t)\|^{2}_{T}\lesssim\mathcal{N}^{2}

(24)

The algorithm uses
O(kd)+\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\log(FT)
samples, run in
O(kd^{\omega})+\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\log^{2}(FT)
time, and succeeds with probability at least
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
.

Proof.

We apply the algorithm in Theorem
7.5
to obtain an estimation
\widetilde{f}_{0}
with
\poly(k)\log(FT)
samples and
\poly(k)\log^{2}(FT)
running time such that
|\widetilde{f}_{0}-f_{0}|\lesssim(\Delta_{h}+\Delta)\sqrt{T(\Delta_{h}+\Delta)}
holds with probability at least
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
. Notice that
|f_{j}-\widetilde{f}_{0}|\leq|f_{j}-f_{0}|+|\widetilde{f}_{0}-f_{0}|\lesssim(T(\Delta_{h}+\Delta))^{1.5}
.

We consider
x^{\prime}(t)=e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{0}t}x(t)=\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}(f_{j}-\widetilde{f}_{0})t}
. By Lemma
2.3
, there exists a polynomial
P(t)
of degree at most

d=O\left((T\Delta_{h}+T\Delta)^{1.5}+k^{3}\log k+k\log 1/\delta\right)

such that it approximates
x^{\prime}
by

\|P(t)-x^{\prime}(t)\|_{T}\leq\frac{\delta}{4}\|x^{\prime}(t)\|_{T}=\frac{\delta}{4}\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}.

which indicates
\|Q(t)-e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{0}t}\cdot x^{*}(t)\|_{T}\leq\frac{\delta}{4}\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}
.

Because we can sample
x(t)
, we can also sample
e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{0}t}\cdot x(t)=Q(t)+g^{\prime}(t)
for
g^{\prime}(t)=e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{0}t}\cdot g(t)+(e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{0}t}\cdot x^{*}(t)-Q(t))
. Hence we apply the algorithm in Theorem
4.5
and choose
R=O(k)
in that proof. Then Procedure
RobustPolynomialLearning
+
takes
O(kd)
samples and
O(kd^{\omega})
time to find a degree
d
polynomial
P(t)
approximating
Q(t)
such that

\|P(t)-Q(t)\|_{T}\lesssim\|g^{\prime}(t)\|_{T},

holds with probability at least
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
. It indicates

\|P(t)-e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{0}t}\cdot x^{*}(t)\|_{T}\lesssim\|P(t)-Q(t)\|_{T}+\|Q(t)-x^{*}(t)\|\lesssim\delta\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}+\|g(t)\|_{T}\eqsim\mathcal{N}.

Therefore we know
\|e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{0}t}\cdot P(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\mathcal{N}^{2}
.
∎

8.2
Bounding the Gram matrix determinant

We define Gram matrix for
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{1}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{2}t},\cdots,e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k}t}
and provide lower/upper bounds for its determinant.

Definition 8.2

(Gram matrix)
.

We define
\Gram_{f_{1},\cdots,f_{k}}
to be

\begin{bmatrix}\langle e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{1}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{1}t}\rangle_{T}&\langle e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{1}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{2}t}\rangle_{T}&\cdots&\langle e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{1}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k}t}\rangle_{T}\\
\langle e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{2}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{1}t}\rangle_{T}&\langle e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{2}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{2}t}\rangle_{T}&\cdots&\langle e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{2}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k}t}\rangle_{T}\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
\langle e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{1}t}\rangle_{T}&\langle e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{2}t}\rangle_{T}&\cdots&\langle e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k}t}\rangle_{T}\\
\end{bmatrix}

Note that the above matrix is a Hermitian matrix with complex entries, thus both its determinant and all eigenvalues are in
\mathbb{R}
.

We defer the proof of the following Theorem to Appendix
A.1
.

Theorem 8.3
.

For real numbers
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}
, let
G_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}}
be the matrix whose
(i,j)
-entry is

\int_{-1}^{1}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}(\xi_{i}-\xi_{j})t}dt.

Then

\det(G_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}})=2^{\tilde{O}(k^{2})}\prod_{i<j}\min(|\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|^{2},1).

We use the following corollary in this section.

Corollary 8.4
.

There exists a universal constant
\alpha>0
such that, for any
T>0
and real numbers
f_{1},\cdots,f_{k}
, the
k\times k
Gram matrix of
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{1}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{2}t},\cdots,e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k}t}
whose
(i,j)
-entry is

\Gram_{f_{1},\cdots,f_{k}}(i,j)=\langle e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{i}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}\rangle_{T}=\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}(f_{i}-f_{j})t}\mathrm{d}t.

satisfies

k^{-\alpha k^{2}}\prod_{i<j}\min((|f_{i}-f_{j}|T)^{2},1)\leq\det\left(\Gram_{f_{1},\cdots,f_{k}}\right)\leq k^{\alpha k^{2}}\prod_{i<j}\min((|f_{i}-f_{j}|T)^{2},1).

Based on Corollary
8.4
, we show the coefficients of a
k
-Fourier-sparse signal can be upper bounded by the energy
\|x\|^{2}_{T}
.

Lemma
2.2
.

There exists a universal constant
c>0
such that for any
x(t)=\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
with frequency gap
\eta=\underset{i\neq j}{\min}|f_{i}-f_{j}|
,

\|x(t)\|^{2}_{T}\geq k^{-ck^{2}}\min\left((\eta T)^{2k},1\right)\sum_{j=1}^{k}|v_{j}|^{2}.

Proof.

Let
\vec{v_{i}}
denote the vector
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{i}t}
and
V=\{\vec{v_{1}},\cdots,\vec{v_{k}}\}
. Notice that
\|\vec{v_{i}}\|_{T}^{2}=\langle\vec{v_{i}},\vec{v_{i}}\rangle=1
. For each
\vec{v_{i}}
, we define
\vec{v}_{i}^{\parallel}
to be the projection of
\vec{v_{i}}
into the linear subspace
\mathrm{span}\{V\setminus\vec{v_{i}}\}=\mathrm{span}\{\vec{v}_{1},\cdots,\vec{v}_{i-1},\vec{v}_{i+1},\cdots,\vec{v}_{k}\}
and
\vec{v}_{i}^{\perp}=\vec{v}_{i}-\vec{v}_{i}^{\parallel}
which is orthogonal to
\mathrm{span}\{V\setminus\vec{v_{i}}\}
by the definition.

Therefore from the orthogonality,

\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}\geq\max_{j\in[k]}\{|v_{j}|^{2}\cdot\|\vec{v}_{j}^{\perp}\|_{T}^{2}\}\geq\frac{1}{k}\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}|v_{j}|^{2}\cdot\|\vec{v}_{j}^{\perp}\|_{T}^{2}.

It is enough to estimate
\|\vec{v}_{j}^{\perp}\|_{T}^{2}
from Claim
3.12
:

\|\vec{v}_{j}^{\perp}\|_{T}^{2}=\frac{\det(\Gram(V))}{\det(\Gram(V\setminus\vec{v}_{i}))}\geq k^{-2\alpha k^{2}}\prod_{j\neq i}\min\left((f_{j}-f_{i})T,1\right)^{2}\geq k^{-2\alpha k^{2}}(\eta T)^{2k-2},

where we use Corollary
8.4
to lower bound it in the last step.
∎

8.3
Perturbing the frequencies does not change the subspace much

We show that for a
k
-Fourier-sparse signal with unboundedly close frequency gap, there always exists another
k
-Fourier-sparse signal with slightly separated gap.

Lemma 8.5

(Slightly Shifting one Frequency)
.

There is a universal constant
C_{0}>0
such that for any
x(t)=\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
and any frequency
f_{k+1}
, there always exists

x^{\prime}(t)=\overset{k-1}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v^{\prime}_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}+v^{\prime}_{k+1}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k+1}t}

with
k
coefficients
v^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime}_{2},\cdots,v^{\prime}_{k-1},v_{k+1}^{\prime}
satisfying

\|x^{\prime}(t)-x(t)\|_{T}\leq k^{C_{0}k^{2}}\cdot\left(|f_{k}-f_{k+1}|T\right)\cdot\|x(t)\|_{T}

Proof.

We abuse the notation
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
to denote a vector in the linear subspace. We plan to shift
f_{k}
to
f_{k+1}
and define

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{1}t},\cdots,e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k-1}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k}t}\}

\displaystyle V^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{1}t},\cdots,e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k-1}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k+1}t}\}

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{1}t},\cdots,e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k-1}t}\}

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{1}t},\cdots,e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k-1}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k+1}t}\}

where
f_{1},f_{2},\cdots,f_{k}
are original frequencies in
x
. The idea is to show that any vector in the linear subspace
\mathrm{span}\{V\}
is close to some vector in the linear subspace
\mathrm{span}\{V^{\prime}\}
.

For convenience, we use
\vec{u}^{\parallel}
to denote the projection of vector
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k}t}
to the linear subspace
\mathrm{span}\{U\}=\mathrm{span}\{e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{1}t},\cdots,e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k-1}t}\}
and
\vec{w}^{\parallel}
denote the projection of vector
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k+1}t}
to this linear subspace
\mathrm{span}\{U\}
. Let
\vec{u}^{\perp}=e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k}t}-\vec{u}^{\parallel}
and
\vec{w}^{\perp}=e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k+1}t}-\vec{w}^{\parallel}
be their orthogonal part to
\mathrm{span}\{U\}
.

From the definition
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k}t}=\vec{u}^{\parallel}+\vec{u}^{\perp}
and
\vec{u}^{\parallel}\in\mathrm{span}\{U\}=\mathrm{span}\{e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{1}t},\cdots,e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k-1}t}\}
, we rewrite the linear combination

x(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}=\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\alpha_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}+v_{k}\cdot\vec{u}^{\perp}

for some scalars
\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{k-1}
.

We will substitute
\vec{u}^{\perp}
by
\vec{w}^{\perp}
in the above linear combination and find a set of new coefficients. Let
\vec{w}^{\perp}=\vec{w}_{1}+\vec{w}_{2}
where
\vec{w}_{1}=\frac{\langle\vec{u}^{\perp},\vec{w}^{\perp}\rangle}{\|\vec{u}^{\perp}\|^{2}_{T}}\vec{u}^{\perp}
is the projection of
\vec{w}^{\perp}
to
\vec{u}^{\perp}
. Therefore
\vec{w}_{2}
is the orthogonal part of the vector
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k+1}t}
to
\mathrm{span}\{V\}=\mathrm{span}\{e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{1}t},\cdots,e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k-1}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k}t}\}
. We use
\delta=\frac{\|\vec{w}_{2}\|_{T}}{\|\vec{w}^{\perp}\|_{T}}
for convenience.

Notice that the
\underset{\beta\in\C}{\min}\frac{\|\vec{u}^{\perp}-\beta\cdot\vec{w}^{\perp}\|_{T}}{\|\vec{u}^{\perp}\|_{T}}=\delta
and
\beta^{*}=\frac{\langle\vec{u}^{\perp},\vec{w}^{\perp}\rangle}{\|\vec{w}^{\perp}\|^{2}_{T}}
is the optimal choice. Therefore we set

x^{\prime}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\beta_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}+v_{k}\cdot\beta^{*}\cdot\vec{w}^{\perp}\in\mathrm{span}\{e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{1}t},\cdots,e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k-1}t},e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{k+1}t}\}

where the coefficients
\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{k-1}
guarantee that the projection of
x^{\prime}
onto
\mathrm{span}\{U\}
is as same as the projection of
x
onto
\mathrm{span}\{U\}
. From the choice of
\beta^{*}
and the definition of
x^{\prime}
,

\|x(t)-x^{\prime}(t)\|_{T}^{2}=\delta^{2}\cdot|v_{k}|^{2}\cdot\|\vec{u}^{\perp}\|_{T}^{2}\leq\delta^{2}\cdot\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

Eventually, we show an upper bound for
\delta^{2}
from Claim
3.12
.

\displaystyle\delta^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\|\vec{w}_{2}\|_{T}^{2}}{\|\vec{w}^{\perp}\|_{T}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\det(\Gram_{W})}{\det(\Gram_{V})}/\frac{\det(\Gram_{V^{\prime}})}{\det(\Gram_{U})}\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ Claim\penalty\ \ref{cla:orthogonal_distance}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\det(\Gram_{W})}{\det(\Gram_{V})}\cdot\frac{\det(\Gram_{U})}{\det(\Gram_{V^{\prime}})}\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ Corollary\penalty\ \ref{cor:determinant_corollary} }

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle k^{4\alpha k^{2}}\cdot\frac{\overset{k+1}{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}\overset{k+1}{\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}{\prod}}\min(|f_{i}-f_{j}|T,1)}{\overset{k}{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}\overset{k}{\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}{\prod}}\min(|f_{i}-f_{j}|T,1)}\cdot\frac{\overset{k-1}{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}\overset{k-1}{\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}{\prod}}\min(|f_{i}-f_{j}|T,1)}{\overset{k-1}{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}\overset{k-1}{\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}{\prod}}\min(|f_{i}-f_{j}|T,1)\cdot\overset{k-1}{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}\min(|f_{i}-f_{k+1}|^{2}T^{2},1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k^{4\alpha k^{2}}|f_{k}-f_{k+1}|^{2}T^{2}

∎

Lemma 8.6
.

For any
k
frequencies
f_{1}<f_{2}<\cdots<f_{k}
, there exists
k
frequencies
f^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,f^{\prime}_{k}
such that
\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\min}f^{\prime}_{i+1}-f^{\prime}_{i}\geq\eta
and for all
i\in[k]
,
|f^{\prime}_{i}-f_{i}|\leq k\eta
.

Proof.

We define the new frequencies
f_{i}^{\prime}
as follows:
f_{1}^{\prime}=f_{1}
and
f_{i}^{\prime}=\max\{f^{\prime}_{i-1}+\eta,f_{i}\}
for
i\in\{2,3,\cdots,k\}
.
∎

8.4
Existence of nearby
k
-Fourier-sparse signal with frequency gap bounded away from zero

We combine the results in the above section to finish the proof of Lemma
2.3
. We first prove that for any
x^{*}(t)=\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
, there always exists another
k
-Fourier-sparse signal
x^{\prime}
close to
x^{*}(t)=\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
such that the frequency gap in
x^{\prime}
is at least
\eta\geq 2^{-\poly(k)}
. Then we show how to find a low degree polynomial
P(t)
approximating
x^{\prime}(t)
.

Lemma
2.1
.

There is a universal constant
C_{1}>0
such that, for any
x^{*}(t)=\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
and any
\delta>0
, there always exist
\eta\geq\frac{\delta}{T}\cdot k^{-C_{1}k^{2}}
and
x^{\prime}(t)=\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v^{\prime}_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f^{\prime}_{j}t}
satisfying

\|x^{\prime}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}\leq\delta\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}

with
\underset{i\neq j}{\min}|f^{\prime}_{i}-f^{\prime}_{j}|\geq\eta
and
\underset{j\in[k]}{\max}\{|f^{\prime}_{j}-f_{j}|\}\leq k\eta
.

Proof.

Using Lemma
8.6
on frequencies
f_{1},\cdots,f_{k}
, we obtain
k
new frequencies
f^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,f^{\prime}_{k}
such that their gap is at least
\eta
and
\max_{i}|f_{i}-f^{\prime}_{i}|\leq k\eta
. Next we use the hybrid argument to find
x^{\prime}
.

Let
x^{(0)}(t)=x^{*}(t)
. For
i=1,\cdots,t,
we apply Lemma
8.5
to shift
f_{i}
to
f^{\prime}_{i}
and obtain

x^{(i)}(t)=\overset{k}{\underset{j=i+1}{\sum}}v^{(i)}_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}+\overset{i}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v^{(i)}_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f^{\prime}_{j}t}.

From Lemma
8.5
, we know
\|x^{(i)}(t)-x^{(i-1)}(t)\|_{T}\leq k^{C_{0}k^{2}}(|f_{i}-f^{\prime}_{i}|T)\|x^{(i-1)}\|_{T}
. Thus we obtain

\left(1-k^{C_{0}k^{2}}(k\eta T)\right)^{i}\|x^{(0)}(t)\|_{T}\leq\|x^{(i)}(t)\|_{T}\leq\left(1+k^{C_{0}k^{2}}(k\eta T)\right)^{i}\|x^{(0)}(t)\|_{T},

which is between
\left[\left(1-i\cdot k^{C_{0}k^{2}}(k\eta T)\right)\|x^{(0)}(t)\|_{T},\left(1+2i\cdot k^{C_{0}k^{2}}(k\eta T)\right)\|x^{(0)}(t)\|_{T}\right]
for
\eta\leq\frac{1}{5T}\cdot k^{-C_{1}k^{2}}
with some
C_{1}>C_{0}
.

At last, we set
x^{\prime}(t)=x^{(k)}(t)
and bound the distance between
x^{\prime}(t)
and
x^{*}(t)
by

\displaystyle\|x^{(k)}(t)-x^{(0)}(t)\|_{T}\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{k}\|x^{(i)}(t)-x^{(i-1)}(t)\|_{T}

by triangle inequality

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{k}k^{C_{0}k^{2}}(|f_{i}-f_{i}^{\prime}|T)\|x^{(i-1)}(t)\|_{T}

by Lemma
8.5

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{k}2k^{C_{0}k^{2}}(k\eta T)\|x^{(i-1)}(t)\|_{T}

by
\underset{i}{\max}|f_{i}-f_{i}^{\prime}|\leq k\eta

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ k\cdot 2k^{C_{0}k^{2}}(k\eta T)\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \delta\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}

where the last inequality follows by the sufficiently small
\eta
.
∎

8.5
Approximating
k
-Fourier-sparse signals by polynomials

For any
k
-Fourier-sparse signal with frequency gap bounded away from
zero, we show that there exists a low degree polynomial which is close
to the original
k
-Fourier-sparse signal in
\|\cdot\|_{T}

distance.

Lemma 8.7

(Existence of low degree polynomial)
.

Let
x^{*}(t)=\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
, where
\forall j\in[k],|f_{j}|\leq\Delta
and
\underset{i\neq j}{\min}|f_{i}-f_{j}|\geq\eta
. There exists a polynomial
Q(t)
of degree

d=O\left(T\Delta+k\log 1/(\eta T)+k^{2}\log k+k\log(1/\delta)\right)

such that,

\|Q(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq\delta\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

(25)

Proof.

For each frequency
f_{j}
, let
Q_{j}(t)=\overset{d-1}{\underset{k=0}{\sum}}\frac{(2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t)^{k}}{k!}
be the first
d
terms in the Taylor Expansion of
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
. For any
t\in[0,T]
, we know the difference between
Q_{j}(t)
and
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
is at most

|Q_{j}(t)-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}|\leq|\frac{(2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}T)^{d}}{d!}|\leq(\frac{2\pi T\Delta\cdot e}{d})^{d}.

We define

Q(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}Q_{j}(t)

and bound the distance between
Q
and
x^{*}
from the above estimation:

\displaystyle\|Q(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}|Q(t)-x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}|\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}(Q_{j}(t)-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t})|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 2k\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}|v_{j}|^{2}\cdot|Q_{j}(t)-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

by triangle inequality

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ k\sum_{j=1}^{k}|v_{j}|^{2}\cdot(\frac{2\pi T\Delta\cdot e}{d})^{2d}

by Taylor expansion

On the other hand, from Lemma
2.2
, we know

\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\geq(\eta T)^{2k}\cdot k^{-ck^{2}}\sum_{j}|v_{j}|^{2}.

Because
d=10\cdot\pi e(T\Delta+k\log 1/(\eta T)+k^{2}\log k+k\log(1/\delta))
is large enough, we have
k(\frac{2\pi T\Delta\cdot e}{d})^{2d}\leq\delta(\eta T)^{2k}\cdot k^{-ck^{2}}
, which indicates that
\|Q(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq\delta\|x^{*}\|_{T}^{2}
from all discussion above. ∎

8.6
Transferring degree-
d
polynomial to (
d
+
1
)-Fourier-sparse signal

In this section, we show how to transfer a degree-
d
polynomial to (
d
+
1
)-Fourier-sparse signal.

Lemma 8.8
.

For any degree-
d
polynomial
Q(t)=\overset{d}{\underset{j=0}{\sum}}c_{j}t^{j}
, any
T>0
and any
\epsilon>0
, there always exist
\gamma>0
and

x^{*}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\alpha_{i}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}(\gamma i)t}

with some coefficients
\alpha_{0},\cdots,\alpha_{d}
such that

\forall t\in[0,T],|x^{*}(t)-Q(t)|\leq\epsilon.

Proof.

We can rewrite
x^{*}(t)
,

\displaystyle x^{*}(t)=\penalty\

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\alpha_{i}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\gamma it}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\alpha_{i}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2\pi\mathbf{i}\gamma it)^{j}}{j!}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2\pi\mathbf{i}\gamma t)^{j}}{j!}\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\alpha_{i}\cdot i^{j}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{d}\frac{(2\pi\mathbf{i}\gamma t)^{j}}{j!}\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\alpha_{i}\cdot i^{j}+\sum_{j=d+1}^{\infty}\frac{(2\pi\mathbf{i}\gamma t)^{j}}{j!}\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\alpha_{i}\cdot i^{j}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle Q(t)+\underbrace{\left(\sum_{j=0}^{d}\frac{(2\pi\mathbf{i}\gamma t)^{j}}{j!}\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\alpha_{i}\cdot i^{j}-Q(t)\right)}_{C_{1}}+\underbrace{\left(\sum_{j=d+1}^{\infty}\frac{(2\pi\mathbf{i}\gamma t)^{j}}{j!}\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\alpha_{i}\cdot i^{j}\right)}_{C_{2}}.

Our goal is to show there exists some parameter
\gamma
and coefficients
\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{d}\}
such that the term
C_{1}=0
and
|C_{2}|\leq\epsilon
. Let’s consider
C_{1}
,

\displaystyle C_{1}=\sum_{j=0}^{d}(\frac{t}{T})^{j}\left(\frac{(2\pi\mathbf{i}\gamma T)^{j}}{j!}\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\alpha_{i}i^{j}-c_{j}\right)

To guarantee
C_{1}=0
, we need to solve a linear system with
d+1
unknown variables and
d+1
constraints,

Find

\displaystyle\penalty\ \alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\cdots\alpha_{d+1}

s.t.

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{(2\pi\mathbf{i}\gamma T)^{j}}{j!}\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\alpha_{i}i^{j}-c_{j}=0,\forall j\in\{0,1,\cdots,d\}

Define
c^{\prime}_{j}=c_{j}j!/(2\pi\mathbf{i}\gamma)^{j}
, let
\alpha
and
c^{\prime}
be the length-(
d+1
) column vectors with
\alpha_{i}
and
c^{\prime}_{j}
. Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1\times d+1}
denote the Vandermonde matrix where
A_{i,j}=i^{j},\forall i,j\in[d+1]\times\{0,1,\cdots,d\}
. Then we need to guarantee
A\alpha=c^{\prime}
. Using the definition of determinant,
\det(A)=\underset{i<j}{\prod}|i-j|\leq 2^{O(d^{2}\log d)}
. Thus
\sigma_{\max}(A)\leq 2^{O(d^{2}\log d)}
and then

\displaystyle\sigma_{\min}(A)=\frac{\det(A)}{\prod_{i=1}^{d-1}\sigma_{i}}\geq 2^{-O(d^{3}\log d)}.

We show how to upper bound
|\alpha_{i}|
,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \max_{i\in[d+1]}|\alpha_{i}|\leq\|\alpha\|_{2}=\|A^{\dagger}c^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq\|A^{\dagger}\|_{2}\cdot\|c^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{\sigma_{\min}(A)}\sqrt{d+1}\max_{0\leq j\leq d}\frac{|c_{j}|j!}{(2\pi\gamma T)^{j}}

Plugging the above equation into
C_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle|C_{2}|

\displaystyle\penalty\ =\left|\sum_{j=d+1}^{\infty}\frac{(2\pi\mathbf{i}\gamma t)^{j}}{j!}\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\alpha_{i}\cdot i^{j}\right|

\displaystyle\penalty\ \leq\sum_{j=d+1}^{\infty}\frac{(2\pi\gamma t)^{j}}{j!}\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}|\alpha_{i}|\cdot i^{j}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \leq\sum_{j=d+1}^{\infty}\frac{(2\pi\gamma t)^{j}}{j!}(d+1)^{d+1}\max_{i\in[d+1]}|\alpha_{i}|

\displaystyle\penalty\ \leq\sum_{j=d+1}^{\infty}\frac{(2\pi\gamma t)^{j}}{j!}(d+1)^{d+2}\frac{1}{\sigma_{\min}(A)}\frac{d!}{(2\pi\gamma T)^{d}}\max_{0\leq j\leq d}|c_{j}|

\displaystyle\penalty\ \leq\epsilon

where the last step follows by choosing sufficiently small

\displaystyle\gamma\lesssim\epsilon/\left(T2^{\Theta(d^{3}\log d)}\underset{0\leq j\leq d}{\max}|c_{j}|\right).

∎

9
k
-cluster Signal Recovery

9.1
Overview

In this section, we prove Lemma
9.1
as the main technical lemma to finish the proof of main Theorem
1.1
, which shows how to learn
x^{*}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
with noise.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
x^{*}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
and
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
be our observation. For any
\delta>0
and
T>0
, let
\mathcal{N}^{2}:=\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}|g(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t+\delta\cdot\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t
. For
\Delta=\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))/T
, Procedure
SignalRecoveryKCluster
+
in Algorithm
8
takes
l=O(k)
frequencies
\widetilde{f}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{f}_{l}
as input and finds
l
polynomials
Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{l}
of degree
d=O((T\Delta)^{1.5}+k^{3}\log k+k\log 1/\delta)
such that

\widetilde{x}(t)=\sum_{j\in[l]}Q_{j}(t)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{j}t}\mathrm{\penalty\ satisfies\penalty\ }\|\widetilde{x}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\mathcal{N}^{2}.

(26)

The procedure succeeds with probability at least
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
, uses
\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\cdot\log(FT)
samples, and runs in
\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\cdot\log^{2}(FT)
time.

For any set
W=\{t_{1},\cdots,t_{m}\}
where each
t_{i}\in[0,T]
, we use

\|\vec{v}\|_{W}=\sqrt{\frac{\sum_{i\in W}|\vec{v}(t_{i})|^{2}}{|W|}}\textit{ for any }\vec{v}:[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}

in this section. We first show that Procedure
SignalRecoveryKCluster
succeeds with constant probability, then prove that Procedure
SignalRecoveryKCluster
+
succeeds with probability at least
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
.

9.2
Heavy clusters separation

Recall the definition of “heavy” clusters.

Definition
2.4
.

Given
x^{*}(t)=\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
, any
\mathcal{N}>0
, and a filter function
(H,\widehat{H})
with bounded support in frequency domain. Let
L_{j}
denote the interval of
\penalty\ \supp(\widehat{e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}\cdot H})
for each
j\in[k]
.

Define an equivalence relation
\sim
on the frequencies
f_{i}
by the transitive closure of the relation
f_{i}\sim f_{j}
if
L_{i}\cap L_{j}\neq\emptyset
. Let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
be the equivalence classes under this relation.

Define
C_{i}=\underset{f\in S_{i}}{\cup}L_{i}
for each
i\in[n]
. We say
C_{i}
is a “heavy” cluster iff
\int_{C_{i}}|\widehat{H\cdot x^{*}}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\cdot\mathcal{N}^{2}/k
.

By reordering
C_{i}
, we can assume
\{C_{1},C_{2},\cdots,C_{l}\}
are heavy clusters, where
l\leq n\leq k
.

Claim
2.5
.

Given
x^{*}(t)=\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
and any
\mathcal{N}>0
, let
H
be the filter function defined in Appendix
C.1
and
C_{1},\cdots,C_{l}
be the heavy clusters from Definition
2.4
. For

S=\left\{j\in[k]\bigg|f_{j}\in C_{1}\cup\cdots C_{l}\right\},

we have
x^{(S)}(t)=\underset{j\in S}{\sum}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
approximating
x^{*}
within distance
\|x^{(S)}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\mathcal{N}^{2}.

Proof.

Let
x^{(\overline{S})}(t)=\underset{j\in[k]\backslash S}{\sum}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
. Notice that
\|x^{*}-x^{(S)}\|_{T}^{2}=\|x^{(\overline{S})}\|^{2}_{T}
.

From the property VI of filter function
(H,\widehat{H})
in Appendix
C.1
, we have

\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|x^{(\overline{S})}(t)\cdot H(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\geq 0.9\int_{0}^{T}|x^{(\overline{S})}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t=0.9\cdot T\|x^{\overline{S}}\|_{T}^{2}.

From Definition
2.4
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|x^{(\overline{S})}(t)\cdot H(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|\widehat{x^{(\overline{S})}\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \int_{[-\infty,+\infty]\setminus C_{1}\cup\cdots\cup C_{l}}|\widehat{x^{*}\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ k\cdot T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k.

Overall, we have
\|x^{(\overline{S})}\|^{2}_{T}\lesssim\mathcal{N}^{2}.

∎

From the guarantee of Theorem
2.6
, for any
j\in S
,
\underset{i\in[l]}{\min}|f_{j}-\widetilde{f}_{i}|\leq\Delta\sqrt{\Delta T}
. From now on, we focus on the recovery of
x^{(S)}
, which is enough to approximate
x^{*}
from the above claim. Because we are looking for
\tilde{x}
approximating
x^{(S)}
within distance
O(\mathcal{N}^{2})
, from Lemma
2.1
, we can assume there is a frequency gap
\eta\geq\frac{\delta}{10T}k^{-O(k^{2})}
among
x^{(S)}
.

9.3
Approximating clusters by polynomials

In this section, we show how to approximate
x^{(S)}
by
x^{\prime}(t)=\sum_{i\in[l]}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)
where
P_{1},\cdots,P_{l}
are low degree polynomials.

Claim 9.2
.

For any
x^{(S)}(t)=\sum_{j\in S}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
with a frequency gap
\eta=\underset{i\neq j}{\min}|f_{i}-f_{j}|
and
l
frequencies
\widetilde{f}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{f}_{l}
with the property
\forall j\in S,\min_{i\in[l]}|f_{j}-\widetilde{f}_{i}|\leq\Delta\sqrt{\Delta T}
, let

d=5\pi\left((T\Delta)^{1.5}+k^{3}\log k+\log 1/\delta\right)\textit{ and }V=\left\{t^{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}|i\in[l],j\in\{0,\cdots,d\}\right\}.

There exists
x^{\prime}(t)\in\mathrm{span}\{V\}
that approximates
x^{(S)}(t)
as follows:

\forall t\in[0,T],|x^{\prime}(t)-x^{(S)}(t)|\leq\delta\|x^{(S)}\|_{T}.

Proof.

From Lemma
2.2
, we know

\|x^{(S)}\|^{2}_{T}\geq(\eta T)^{2k}\cdot k^{-ck^{2}}\sum_{j\in S}|v_{j}|^{2}.

For each frequency
f_{j}
, we use
p_{j}
to denote the index in
[l]
such that
|f_{j}-\widetilde{f}_{p_{j}}|\leq\Delta\sqrt{\Delta T}
. We rewrite

x^{(S)}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{l}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}}\left(\sum_{j\in S:p_{j}=i}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}(f_{j}-\widetilde{f}_{i})t}\right).

For
d=5\pi((T\Delta)^{1.5}+k^{3}\log k+\log 1/\delta)
and each
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}(f_{j}-\widetilde{f}_{p_{j}})t}
, let
Q_{j}(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\frac{\left(2\pi\mathbf{i}(f_{j}-\widetilde{f}_{p_{j}})t\right)^{i}}{i!}
be the first
d
terms in the Taylor Expansion of
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}(f_{j}-\widetilde{f}_{p_{j}})t}
. For any
t\in[0,T]
, we know the difference between
Q_{j}(t)
and
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}(f_{j}-\widetilde{f}_{p_{j}})t}
is at most

\forall t\in[0,T],|Q_{j}(t)-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}(f_{j}-\widetilde{f}_{p_{j}})t}|\leq|\frac{(2\pi\mathbf{i}(f_{j}-\widetilde{f}_{p_{j}})T)^{d}}{d!}|\leq(\frac{8\pi(\Delta T)^{1.5}}{d})^{d}.

Let
x^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{l}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}\left(\sum_{j\in S:p_{j}=i}v_{j}Q_{j}(t)\right).
From all discussion above, we know for any
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle|x^{\prime}(t)-x^{(S)}(t)|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \left(\sum_{j\in S}|v_{j}|(\frac{8\pi(T\Delta)^{1.5}}{d})^{d}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ k(\frac{8\pi(T\Delta)^{1.5}}{d})^{2d}\sum_{j}|v_{j}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{k(\frac{8\pi(T\Delta)^{1.5}}{d})^{2d}}{(\eta T)^{2k}\cdot k^{-ck^{2}}}\|x^{(S)}\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \delta^{2}\|x^{(S)}\|_{T}^{2}.

∎

We provide a property of functions in
\mathrm{span}\{V\}
such that we can use the Chernoff bound and the
\epsilon
-net argument on vectors in
\mathrm{span}\{V\}
.

Claim
2.7
.

For any
\vec{u}\in\mathrm{span}\left\{e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}\cdot t^{j}\bigg|j\in\{0,\cdots,d\},i\in[l]\right\}
, there exists some universal constants
C_{1}\leq 4
and
C_{2}\leq 3
such that

\max_{t\in[0,T]}\{|\vec{u}(t)|^{2}\}\lesssim(ld)^{C_{1}}\log^{C_{2}}(ld)\cdot\|\vec{u}\|^{2}_{T}

Proof.

From Lemma
8.8
, we can approximate each polynomial in
\vec{u}
by a linear combination of
\{1,e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\cdot\gamma t},\cdots,e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\cdot(\gamma d)t}\}
such that we obtain
u^{*}\in\mathrm{span}\left\{e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\cdot(\gamma j)t}\cdot e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\tilde{f}_{i}t}|i\in[l],j\in\{0,\cdots,d+1\}\right\}
for some small
\gamma
such that
\forall t\in[0,T],|\vec{u}(t)-u^{*}(t)|\leq 0.01\|\vec{u}\|_{T}
.

From Lemma
5.1
, we know

\max_{t\in[0,T]}|u^{*}(t)|^{2}\leq C\cdot\left((ld+1)^{4}\cdot\log^{3}(ld+1)\right)\|u^{*}\|_{T}^{2}.

For some constant
C^{\prime}
, we have

\max_{t\in[0,T]}|\vec{u}(t)|^{2}\leq C^{\prime}\left((kd)^{C_{1}}\log^{C_{2}}d\right)\|\vec{u}\|_{T}^{2}.

∎

9.4
Main result, with constant success probability

In this section, we show that the output
\widetilde{x}
is close to
x^{\prime}
with high probability using the
\epsilon
-net argument, which is enough to prove
\|\widetilde{x}-x\|_{T}\lesssim\mathcal{N}^{2}
from all discussion above. Because we can prove Lemma
9.6
(which is the main goal of this section), then combining
\|x^{\prime}-x^{*}\|_{T}\leq\|x^{\prime}-x^{(S)}\|_{T}+\|x^{(S)}-x^{*}\|_{T}\lesssim\delta\|x^{*}\|_{T}
and Lemma
9.6
, we have
\|x^{*}-\widetilde{x}\|_{T}\lesssim\|g\|_{T}+\delta\|x^{*}\|_{T}
, which finishes the proof of Procedure
SignalRecoveryKCluster
in Algorithm
8
achieving the Equation (
26
) with constant success probability but not
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
. We will boost the success probability in Section
9.5
.

We first provide an
\epsilon
-net
\mathcal{P}
for the unit vectors
\mathcal{Q}=\{\vec{u}\in\mathrm{span}\{V\}\big|\|\vec{u}\|_{T}^{2}=1\}
in the linear subspace
\mathrm{span}\{V\}
where
V=\left\{t^{j}\cdot e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}\big|j\in\{0,1,\cdots,d\},i\in[l]\right\}
from the above discussion. Notice that the dimension of
\mathrm{span}\{V\}
is at most
l(d+1)
.

Claim 9.3
.

There exists an
\epsilon
-net
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathrm{span}\{V\}
such that

1.

\forall\vec{u}\in Q,\exists\vec{w}\in\mathcal{P},\|\vec{u}-\vec{w}\|_{T}\leq\epsilon.

2.

|\mathcal{P}|\leq\left(5\frac{l(d+1)}{\epsilon}\right)^{2l(d+1)}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P^{\prime}}
be an
\frac{\epsilon}{l(d+1)}
-net in the unit circle of
\mathbb{C}
with size at most
(4\frac{l(d+1)}{\epsilon}+1)^{2}
, i.e.,

\mathcal{P^{\prime}}=\left\{\frac{\epsilon}{2l(d+1)}j_{1}+\mathbf{i}\frac{\epsilon}{2l(d+1)}j_{2}\bigg|j_{1},j_{2}\in\mathbb{Z},|j_{1}|\leq\frac{2l(d+1)}{\epsilon},|j_{2}|\leq\frac{2l(d+1)}{\epsilon}\right\}.

Observe that the dimension of
\mathrm{span}\{V\}
is at most
l(d+1)
. Then we take an orthogonal basis
\vec{w}_{1},\cdots,\vec{w}_{l(d+1)}
in
\mathrm{span}\{V\}
and set

P=\{\sum_{i=1}^{l(d+1)}\alpha_{i}\vec{w}_{i}\big|\forall i\in[l(d+1)],\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{P^{\prime}}\}.

Therefore
\mathcal{P}
is an
\epsilon
-net for
Q
and
|\mathcal{P}|\leq\left(5\frac{l(d+1)}{\epsilon}\right)^{2l(d+1)}
.
∎

We first prove that
W
is a good estimation for all functions in the
\epsilon
-net
\mathcal{P}
.

Claim 9.4
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a universal constant
C_{3}\leq 5
such that for a set
S
of i.i.d. samples chosen uniformly at random over
[0,T]
of size
|S|\geq\frac{3(kd)^{C_{3}}\log^{C_{3}}d/\epsilon}{\epsilon^{2}}
,then with probability at least
1-k^{-k}
, for all
\vec{w}\in\mathcal{P}
, we have

\|\vec{w}\|_{W}\in\left[(1-\epsilon)\|\vec{w}\|_{T},(1+\epsilon)\|\vec{w}\|_{T}\right].

Proof.

From Claim
2.7
and Lemma
3.5
, for each
\vec{w}\in\mathcal{P}
,

\mathsf{Pr}\left[\|\vec{w}(t)\|_{W}\notin\big[(1-\epsilon)\|\vec{w}\|_{T},(1+\epsilon)\|\vec{w}\|_{T}\big]\right]\leq 2^{-\frac{|W|\epsilon^{2}}{3(kd)^{C_{1}}\log^{C_{2}+0.5}d}}\leq 2^{-kd\log^{1.5}\frac{d}{\epsilon}}.

From the union bound,
\|\vec{w}\|_{W}\in[(1-\epsilon)\|\vec{w}\|_{T},(1+\epsilon)\|\vec{w}\|_{T}]
for any
\vec{w}\in\mathcal{P}
with probability at least
1-(\frac{d}{\epsilon})^{-kd\log^{0.5}d}\cdot|\mathcal{P}|\geq 1-d^{-d}
.
∎

Then We prove that
W
is a good estimation for all functions in
\mathrm{span}\{V\}
using the property of
\epsilon
-nets.

Claim 9.5
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a universal constant
C_{3}\leq 5
such that for a set
W
of i.i.d. samples chosen uniformly at random over
[0,T]
of size
|W|\geq\frac{3(kd)^{C_{3}}\log^{C_{3}}d/\epsilon}{\epsilon^{2}}
,then with probability at least
1-d^{-d}
, for all
u\in\mathrm{span}\{V\}
, we have

\|\vec{u}\|_{W}\in\left[(1-3\epsilon)\|\vec{u}\|_{T},(1+3\epsilon)\|\vec{u}\|_{T}\right]

Proof.

We assume that the above claim is true for any
\vec{w}\in\mathcal{P}
. Without loss of generality, we consider
\vec{u}\in\mathcal{Q}
such that
\|\vec{u}\|_{T}=1
.

Let
\vec{w}_{0}
be the vector in
\mathcal{P}
that minimizes
\|\vec{w}-\vec{u}\|_{T}
for all
\vec{w}\in\mathcal{P}
, i.e.,
\vec{w}_{0}=\underset{\vec{w}\in\mathcal{P}}{\arg\min}\|\vec{w}-\vec{u}\|_{T}
. Define
\vec{u}_{1}=\vec{u}-\vec{w}_{0}
and notice that
\|\vec{u}_{1}\|_{T}\leq\epsilon
because
\mathcal{P}
is a
\epsilon
-net. If
\|\vec{u}_{1}\|_{T}=0
, then we skip the rest of this procedure. Otherwise, we define
\alpha_{1}=\|\vec{u}_{1}\|_{T}
and normalize
\widetilde{u}_{1}=\vec{u}_{1}/\alpha_{1}
.

Then we choose
\vec{w}_{1}
to be the vector in
\mathcal{P}
that minimizes
\|\vec{w}-\widetilde{u}_{1}\|_{T}
for all
\vec{w}\in\mathcal{P}
. Similarly, we set
\vec{u}_{2}=\widetilde{u}_{1}-\vec{w}_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}=\|\vec{u}_{2}\|_{T}
. Next we repeat this process for
\widetilde{u}_{2}=\vec{u}_{2}/\alpha_{2}
and so on. The recursive definition can be summarized in the following sense,

initial :

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{0}=\vec{u}\text{ and }m=10\log_{1/\epsilon}(ld)+1,

\displaystyle\text{For }i\in\{0,1,2,\cdots,m\}\penalty\ :

\displaystyle\vec{w}_{i}=\underset{\vec{w}\in\mathcal{P}}{\arg\min}\|\vec{w}-\widetilde{u}_{i}\|_{T},

\displaystyle\vec{u}_{i+1}=\widetilde{u}_{i}-\vec{w}_{i}\text{ and }\alpha_{i+1}=\|\vec{u}_{i+1}\|_{T},

\displaystyle\text{if }\alpha_{i+1}=0,\text{\penalty\ stop.}

\displaystyle\text{if }\alpha_{i+1}\neq 0,\widetilde{u}_{i+1}=\vec{u}_{i+1}/\alpha_{i+1}\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ continue},

Eventually, we have
\vec{u}=\vec{w}_{0}+\alpha_{1}\vec{w}_{1}+\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\vec{w}_{2}+\cdots+\prod_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}(\vec{w}_{m}+\vec{u}_{m+1})
where each
|\alpha_{i}|\leq\epsilon
and each
\vec{w}_{i}
is in the
\epsilon
-net
\mathcal{P}
. Notice that
\|\vec{u}_{m+1}\|_{T}\leq 1
and
\|\vec{u}_{m+1}\|_{W}\leq(ld+1)^{3}\cdot\|\vec{u}_{m+1}\|_{T}
from Claim
2.7
. We prove a lower bound for
\|\vec{u}\|_{W}
,

\displaystyle\|\vec{u}\|_{W}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\vec{w}_{0}+\alpha_{1}\vec{w}_{1}+\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\vec{w}_{2}+\cdots+\prod_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}(\vec{w}_{m}+\vec{u}_{m+1})\|_{W}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|\vec{w}_{0}\|_{W}-\|\alpha_{1}\vec{w}_{1}\|_{W}-\|\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\vec{w}_{2}\|_{W}-\cdots-\|\prod_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}\vec{w}_{m}\|_{W}-\|\prod_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}\vec{u}_{m+1}\|_{W}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon)-\epsilon(1+\epsilon)-\epsilon^{2}(1+\epsilon)-\cdots-\epsilon^{m}(1+\epsilon)-\epsilon^{m}\|\vec{u}_{m+1}\|_{W}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 1-\epsilon-\frac{(1+\epsilon)\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}-\epsilon^{m}\cdot(ld+1)^{3}\geq 1-3\epsilon.

Similarly, we have
\|\vec{u}\|_{W}\leq 1+3\epsilon
.
∎

Lemma 9.6
.

With probability at least
0.99
over the
m
i.i.d samples in
W
,

\|x^{\prime}(t)-\widetilde{x}(t)\|_{T}\leq 2200\left(\|g(t)\|_{T}+\|x^{(S)}(t)-x^{\prime}(t)\|_{T}\right).

Proof.

Let
g^{\prime}(t)=g(t)+x^{*}(t)-x^{\prime}(t)
such that
x(t)=x^{\prime}(t)+g^{\prime}(t)
. Then we choose
\epsilon=0.03
and bound:

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|x^{\prime}(t)-\widetilde{x}(t)\|_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1+3\epsilon)\|x^{\prime}(t)-\widetilde{x}(t)\|_{W}\quad

\displaystyle\text{with\penalty\ prob.\penalty\ }1-2^{-\Omega(d\log d)}\penalty\ \text{by\penalty\ Claim\penalty\ \ref{cla:concentration_for_any_polynomial_signal}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ 1.09\|x^{\prime}(t)-\widetilde{x}(t)\|_{W}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }\epsilon=0.03

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ 1.09\|x(t)-g^{\prime}(t)-\widetilde{x}(t)\|_{W}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }x^{\prime}(t)=x(t)-g^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 1.09\|x(t)-\widetilde{x}(t)\|_{W}+1.09\|g^{\prime}(t)\|_{W}\quad

by triangle inequality

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ 1.09\|x(t)-x^{\prime}(t)\|_{W}+1.09\|g^{\prime}(t)\|_{W}\quad

\displaystyle\text{by\penalty\ }\widetilde{x}=\underset{y\in\mathrm{span}\{V\}}{\arg\min}\|x-y\|_{W}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ 2.18\|g^{\prime}(t)\|_{W}.

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }x(t)-x^{\prime}(t)=g(t)

From the fact that
\E_{W}[\|g^{\prime}\|_{W}]=\|g^{\prime}\|_{T}
,
\|g^{\prime}\|_{W}\leq 1000\|g^{\prime}\|_{T}
with probability at least .999. It indicates
\|x^{\prime}(t)-\widetilde{x}(t)\|_{T}\leq 2200\|g^{\prime}\|_{T}
with probability at least
0.99
from all discussion above.
∎

9.5
Boosting the success probability

In order to achieve
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
for the main theorem, we cannot combine Procedure
SignalRecoveryKCluster
with
FrequencyRecoveryKCluster
directly. However, using the similar proof technique in Theorem
4.5
, we are able to boost the success probability by using Procedure
SignalRecoveryKCluster
+
in Algorithm
8
. It runs Procedure
SignalRecoveryKCluster

R=O(k)
times in parallel for independent fresh samples and report
R
different
d
-Fourier-sparse signals
\widetilde{x}_{i}(t)
. Then, taking
m=\poly(k)
new locations
\{t_{1},t_{2},\cdots,t_{m}\}
, and computing
\widetilde{A}
as before and
\widetilde{b}_{j}
by taking the median of
\{\widetilde{x}_{1}(t_{j}),\cdots,\widetilde{x}_{R}(t_{j})\}
. At the end, solving the linear regression for matrix
\widetilde{A}
and vector
\widetilde{b}
. Thus, we complete the proof of Lemma
9.1
.

Because we can transfer a degree-
d
polynomial to a
d
-Fourier-sparse signal by Lemma
8.8
, the output of Procedure
CFTKCluster
in Algorithm
8
matches the main theorem,

Theorem
1.1
.

Let
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
, where
x^{*}
is
k
-Fourier-sparse signal with
frequencies in
[-F,F]
. Given samples of
x
over
[0,T]
we can
output
\widetilde{x}(t)
such that with probability at least
