1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
,

\left\lVert\widetilde{x}-x^{*}\right\rVert_{T}\lesssim\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{T}+\delta\left\lVert x^{*}\right\rVert_{T}.

Our algorithm uses
\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\cdot\log(FT)

samples and
\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))\cdot\log^{2}(FT)
time. The output
\widetilde{x}

is
\poly(k,\log(1/\delta))
-Fourier-sparse signal.

Appendix A
Technical Proofs

A.1
Proof of Theorem
8.3

We prove the following Theorem

Theorem
8.3
.

For real numbers
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}
, let
G_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}}
be the matrix whose
(i,j)
-entry is

\int_{-1}^{1}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}(\xi_{i}-\xi_{j})t}dt.

Then

\det(G_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}})=2^{\tilde{O}(k^{2})}\prod_{i<j}\min(|\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|^{2},1).

First, we note by the Cauchy-Binet formula that the determinant in question is equal to

\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}\ldots\int_{-1}^{1}\left|\det([e^{2\pi i\xi_{i}t_{j}}]_{i,j})\right|^{2}dt_{1}dt_{2}\ldots dt_{k}.

(27)

We next need to consider the integrand in the special case when
\sum|\xi_{i}|\leq 1/8
.

Lemma A.1
.

If
\xi_{i}\in R
and
t_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\sum_{i}|\xi_{i}|(\max_{i}|t_{i}|)\leq 1/8
then

|\det([e^{2\pi i\xi_{i}t_{j}}]_{i,j})|=\Theta\left(\frac{(2\pi)^{\binom{k}{2}}\prod_{i<j}|t_{i}-t_{j}||\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|}{1!2!\cdots k!}\right).

Proof.

Firstly, by adding a constant to all the
t_{j}
we can make them non-negative. This multiplies the determinant by a root of unity, and at most doubles
\sum_{i}|\xi_{i}|(\max_{i}|t_{i}|)
.

By continuity, it suffices to consider the
t_{i}
to all be multiples of
1/N
for some large integer
N
. By multiplying all the
t_{j}
by
N
and all
\xi_{i}
by
1/N
, we may assume that all of the
t_{j}
are non-negative integers with
t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq\ldots\leq t_{k}
.

Let
z_{i}=\exp(2\pi i\xi_{i})
. Then our determinant is

\det\left(\left[z_{i}^{t_{j}}\right]_{i,j}\right),

which is equal to the Vandermonde determinant times the Schur polynomial
s_{\lambda}(z_{i})
where
\lambda
is the partition
\lambda_{j}=t_{j}-(j-1)
.

Therefore, this determinant equals

\prod_{i<j}(z_{i}-z_{j})s_{\lambda}(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{k}).

The absolute value of

\prod_{i<j}(z_{i}-z_{j})

is approximately
\prod_{i<j}(2\pi i)(\xi_{i}-\xi_{j})
, which has absolute value
(2\pi)^{\binom{k}{2}}\prod_{i<j}|\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|
. We have left to evaluate the size of the Schur polynomial.

By standard results,
s_{\lambda}
is a polynomial in the
z_{i}
with non-negative coefficients, and all exponents at most
\max_{j}|t_{j}|
in each variable. Therefore, the monomials with non-zero coefficients will all have real part at least
1/2
and absolute value
1
when evaluated at the
z_{i}
. Therefore,

|s_{\lambda}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})|=\Theta(|s_{\lambda}(1,1,\ldots,1)|).

On the other hand, by the Weyl character formula

s_{\lambda}(1,1,\ldots,1)=\prod_{i<j}\frac{t_{j}-t_{i}}{j-i}=\frac{\prod_{i<j}|t_{i}-t_{j}|}{1!2!\ldots k!}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Next we prove our Theorem when the
\xi
have small total variation.

Lemma A.2
.

If there exists a
\xi_{0}
so that
\sum|\xi_{i}-\xi_{0}|<1/8
, then

\det(G_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}})=\Theta\left(\frac{2^{3k(k-1)/2}\pi^{k(k-1)}\prod_{i<j}|\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|^{2}}{(k!)^{3}\prod_{n=0}^{k-1}(2n)!}\right).

Proof.

By translating the
\xi_{i}
we can assume that
\xi_{0}=0
.

By the above we have

\Theta(\frac{(2\pi)^{k(k-1)}\prod_{i<j}|\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|^{2}}{(1!2!\cdots k!)^{2}})\int_{-1}^{1}\ldots\int_{-1}^{1}\prod_{i<j}|t_{i}-t_{j}|^{2}dt_{1}\ldots dt_{k}.

We note that by the Cauchy-Binet formula the latter term is the determinant of the matrix
M
with
M_{i,j}=\int_{-1}^{1}t^{i+j}dt
. This is the Graham matrix associated to the polynomials
t^{i}
for
0\leq i\leq k-1
. Applying Graham-Schmidt (without the renormalization step) to this set yields the basis
P_{n}\alpha_{n}
where
\alpha_{n}=\frac{2^{n}(n!)^{2}}{(2n)!}
is the inverse of the leading term of
P_{n}
. This polynomial has norm
\alpha_{n}^{2}2/(2n+1)
. Therefore, the integral over the
t_{i}
yields

\prod_{n=0}^{k-1}\frac{2^{n+1}(n!)^{2}}{(n+1)(2n)!}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Next we extend this result to the case that all the
\xi
are within
\poly(k)
of each other.

Proposition A.3
.

If there exists a
\xi_{0}
so that
|\xi_{i}-\xi_{0}|=\poly(k)
for all
i
, then

\det(G_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}})=2^{\tilde{O}(k^{2})}\prod_{i<j}\min(|\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|^{2},1).

Proof.

We begin by proving the lower bound. We note that for
0<x<1
,

\det(G_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}})\geq\int_{-x}^{x}\int_{-x}^{x}\ldots\int_{-1}^{1}\left|\det([e^{2\pi i\xi_{i}t_{j}}]_{i,j})\right|^{2}dt_{1}dt_{2}\ldots dt_{k}=x^{k}\det(G_{\xi_{1}/x,\xi_{2}/x,\ldots,\xi_{k}/k}).

Taking
x=1/\poly(k)
, we may apply the above Lemma to compute the determinant on the right hand side, yielding an appropriate lower bound.

To prove the lower bound, we note that we can divide our
\xi_{i}
into clusters,
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, where for any
i,j
in the same cluster
|\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|<1/k
and for
i
and
j
in different clusters
|\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|\geq 1/k^{2}
. We then note as a property of Graham matrices that

\det(G_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}})\leq\prod_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}\det(G_{\{\xi_{j}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}\}})=2^{\tilde{O}(k^{2})}\prod_{i<j,\textrm{ in same cluster}}|\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|^{2}=2^{\tilde{O}(k^{2})}\prod_{i<j}|\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|^{2}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Finally, we are ready to prove our Theorem.

Proof.

Let
I(t)
be the indicator function of the interval
[-1,1]
.

Recall that there is a function
h(t)
so that for any function
f
that is a linear combination of at most
k
complex exponentials that
|h(t)f(t)|_{2}=\Theta(|I(t)f(t)|_{2})
and so that
\hat{h}
is supported on an interval of length
\poly(k)<k^{C}
about the origin.

Note that we can divide our
\xi_{i}
into clusters,
\mathcal{C_{i}}
, so that for
i
and
j
in a cluster
|\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|<k^{C+1}
and for
i
and
j
in different clusters
|\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|>k^{C}
.

Let
\tilde{G}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\ldots,\xi_{k}^{\prime}}
be the matrix with
(i,j)
-entry
\int_{\mathbb{R}}|h(t)|^{2}e^{(2\pi i)(\xi_{i}-\xi_{j})t}dt.

We claim that for any
k^{\prime}\leq k
that

\det(\tilde{G}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\ldots,\xi_{k}^{\prime}})=2^{O(k^{\prime})}\det(G_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\ldots,\xi_{k}^{\prime}}).

This is because both are Graham determinants, one for the set of functions
I(t)\exp((2\pi i)\xi_{j}t)
and the other for
h(t)\exp((2\pi i)\xi_{j}t)
. However since any linear combination of the former has
L^{2}
norm a constant multiple of that the same linear combination of the latter, we have that

\tilde{G}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\ldots,\xi_{k}^{\prime}}=\Theta(G_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\ldots,\xi_{k}^{\prime}})

as self-adjoint matrices. This implies the appropriate bound.

Therefore, we have that

\det(G_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}})=2^{O(k)}\det(\tilde{G}_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}}).

However, note that by the Fourier support of
h
that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|h(t)|^{2}e^{(2\pi i)(\xi_{i}-\xi_{j})t}dt=0

if
|\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|>k^{C}
, which happens if
i
and
j
are in different clusters. Therefore
\tilde{G}
is block diagonal and hence its determinant equals

\det(\tilde{G}_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}})=\prod_{\mathcal{C_{i}}}\det(\tilde{G}_{\{\xi_{j}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}\}})=2^{O(k)}\prod_{\mathcal{C_{i}}}\det(G_{\{\xi_{j}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}\}}).

However the Proposition above shows that

\prod_{\mathcal{C_{i}}}\det(G_{\{\xi_{j}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}\}})=2^{\tilde{O}(k^{2})}\prod_{i<j}\min(1,|\xi_{i}-\xi_{j}|^{2}).

This completes the proof.
∎

A.2
Proofs of Lemma
5.3
and Lemma
5.4

We fix
z_{1},\cdots,z_{k}
to be complex numbers on the unit circle and use
Q(z)
to denote the degree-
k
polynomial
\overset{k}{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}(z-z_{i})
.

Lemma
5.3
.

Let
Q(z)
be a degree
k
polynomial, all of whose roots are complex numbers with absolute value
1
. For any integer
n
, let
r_{n,k}(z)=\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}r_{n,k}^{(l)}\cdot z^{l}
denote the residual polynomial of

r_{n,k}(z)\equiv z^{n}\pmod{Q(z)}.

Then, each coefficient of
r_{n,k}
is bounded:
|r_{n,k}^{(l)}|\leq 2^{k}n^{k-1}
for any
l
.

Proof.

By definition,
r_{n,k}(z_{i})=z_{i}^{n}
. From the polynomial interpolation, we have

r_{n,k}(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\underset{j\in[k]\backslash i}{\prod}(z-z_{j})z_{i}^{n}}{\underset{j\in[k]\backslash i}{\prod}(z_{i}-z_{j})}.

Let
\Sym_{S,i}
be the symmetry polynomial of
z_{1},\cdots,z_{k}
with
degree
i
among subset
S\subseteq[k]
, i.e.,
\Sym_{S,i}=\underset{S^{\prime}\subseteq\binom{S}{i}}{\sum}\underset{j\in S^{\prime}}{\prod}z_{j}
. Then

r_{n,k}^{(l)}=(-1)^{k-1-l}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\Sym_{[k]\setminus i,k-1-l}\cdot z_{i}^{n}}{\underset{j\in[k]\backslash i}{\prod}(z_{i}-z_{j})}.

We omit
(-1)^{k-1-l}
in
the rest of proof and use induction on
n,k,
and
l
to prove

|r_{n,k}^{(l)}|\leq\binom{k-1}{l}\binom{n}{k-1}
.

Base Case of
n
: For any
n<k
, from the definition,
r(z)=z^{n}
and
|r_{n,k}^{(l)}|\leq 1
.

Suppose it is true for any
n<n_{0}
. We consider
r_{n_{0},k}^{l}
from now on. When
k=1
,
r_{n,0}=z_{1}^{n}
is bounded by
1
because
z_{1}
is on the unit circle of
\mathbb{C}
.

Given
n_{0}
, suppose the induction hypothesis is true for any
k<k_{0}
and any
l<k
. For
k=k_{0}
, we first prove that
|r_{n_{0},k_{0}}^{(k_{0}-1)}|\leq\binom{n_{0}}{k_{0}-1}
then prove that
|r_{n_{0},k_{0}}^{(l)}|\leq\binom{k_{0}-1}{l}\binom{n_{0}}{k_{0}-1}
for
l=k_{0}-2,\cdots,0
.

\displaystyle r_{n_{0},k_{0}}^{(k_{0}-1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{z_{i}^{n_{0}}}{\underset{j\in[k_{0}]\backslash i}{\prod}(z_{i}-z_{j})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}-1}\frac{z_{i}^{n_{0}}}{\underset{j\in[k_{0}]\backslash i}{\prod}(z_{i}-z_{j})}+\frac{z_{k_{0}}^{n_{0}}}{\underset{j\in[k_{0}]\backslash{k_{0}}}{\prod}(z_{k_{0}}-z_{j})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}-1}\frac{z_{i}^{n_{0}}-z_{i}^{n_{0}-1}z_{k_{0}}+z_{i}^{n_{0}-1}z_{k_{0}}}{\underset{j\in[k_{0}]\backslash i}{\prod}(z_{i}-z_{j})}+\frac{z_{k_{0}}^{n_{0}}}{\underset{j\in k_{0}\backslash{k_{0}}}{\prod}(z_{k_{0}}-z_{j})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}-1}\left(\frac{z_{i}^{n_{0}-1}}{\underset{j\in[k_{0}-1]\backslash i}{\prod}(z_{i}-z_{j})}+\frac{z_{i}^{n_{0}-1}z_{k_{0}}}{\underset{j\in k_{0}\backslash i}{\prod}(z_{i}-z_{j})}\right)+\frac{z_{k_{0}}^{n_{0}}}{\underset{j\in k_{0}\backslash{k_{0}}}{\prod}(z_{k_{0}}-z_{j})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}-1}\frac{z_{i}^{n_{0}-1}}{\underset{j\in[k_{0}-1]\backslash i}{\prod}(z_{i}-z_{j})}\right)+\left(z_{k_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{z_{i}^{n_{0}-1}}{\underset{j\in k_{0}\backslash i}{\prod}(z_{i}-z_{j})}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{n_{0}-1,k_{0}-1}^{(k_{0}-2)}+z_{k_{0}}\cdot r_{n_{0}-1,k_{0}}^{(k_{0}-1)}

Hence
|r_{n_{0},k_{0}}^{(k_{0}-1)}|\leq|r_{n_{0}-1,[k_{0}-1]}^{(k_{0}-2)}|+|r_{n_{0}-1,k_{0}}^{(k_{0}-1)}|\leq\binom{n_{0}-2}{k_{0}-2}+\binom{n_{0}-2}{k_{0}-1}=\binom{n_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}
. For
l<k_{0}-1
, we have

\displaystyle r_{n_{0},k_{0}}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}}\frac{\Sym_{[k_{0}]\setminus i,k_{0}-1-l}\cdot z_{i}^{n_{0}}}{\underset{j\in[k_{0}]\backslash i}{\prod}(z_{i}-z_{j})}\quad\text{let\penalty\ }l^{\prime}=k_{0}-1-l

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}-1}\frac{\left(\Sym_{[k_{0}-1]\setminus i,l^{\prime}}+\Sym_{[k_{0}-1]\setminus i,l^{\prime}-1}\cdot z_{k_{0}}\right)z_{i}^{n_{0}}}{\underset{j\in[k_{0}]\backslash i}{\prod}(z_{i}-z_{j})}+\frac{\Sym_{[k_{0}-1],l^{\prime}}\cdot z_{k_{0}}^{n_{0}}}{\underset{j<k_{0}}{\prod}(z_{k_{0}}-z_{j})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}-1}\frac{\Sym_{[k_{0}-1]\setminus i,l^{\prime}}\cdot(z_{i}-z_{k_{0}})z_{i}^{n_{0}-1}+\Sym_{[k_{0}-1]\setminus i,l^{\prime}}\cdot z_{k_{0}}z_{i}^{n_{0}-1}+\Sym_{[k_{0}-1]\setminus i,l^{\prime}-1}\cdot z_{k_{0}}z_{i}^{n_{0}}}{\underset{j\in[k_{0}]\backslash i}{\prod}(z_{i}-z_{j})}

\displaystyle+\frac{\Sym_{[k_{0}-1],l^{\prime}}\cdot z_{k_{0}}^{n_{0}}}{\underset{j<k_{0}}{\prod}(z_{k_{0}}-z_{j})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}-1}\frac{\Sym_{[k_{0}-1]\setminus i,l^{\prime}}\cdot(z_{i}-z_{k_{0}})z_{i}^{n_{0}-1}+\Sym_{[k_{0}-1],l^{\prime}}\cdot z_{k_{0}}z_{i}^{n_{0}-1}}{\underset{j\in[k_{0}]\backslash i}{\prod}(z_{i}-z_{j})}+\frac{\Sym_{[k_{0}-1],l^{\prime}}\cdot z_{k_{0}}^{n_{0}}}{\underset{j<k_{0}}{\prod}(z_{k_{0}}-z_{j})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}-1}\frac{\Sym_{[k_{0}-1]\setminus i,l^{\prime}}z_{i}^{n_{0}-1}}{\underset{j\in[k_{0}-1]\backslash i}{\prod}(z_{i}-z_{j})}+\sum_{i=1}^{k_{0}-1}\frac{\Sym_{[k_{0}-1],l^{\prime}}\cdot z_{k_{0}}z_{i}^{n_{0}-1}}{\underset{j\in[k_{0}]\backslash i}{\prod}(z_{i}-z_{j})}+\frac{\Sym_{[k_{0}-1],l^{\prime}}\cdot z_{k_{0}}^{n_{0}}}{\underset{j<k_{0}}{\prod}(z_{k_{0}}-z_{j})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{n_{0}-1,k_{0}-1}^{(l-1)}+\Sym_{[k_{0}-1],k_{0}-1-l}\cdot z_{k_{0}}\cdot r_{n_{0}-1,k_{0}}^{(k_{0}-1)}

By induction hypothesis,
|r_{n_{0},k_{0}}^{(l)}|\leq\binom{k_{0}-2}{l-1}\binom{n_{0}-1}{k_{0}-2}+\binom{k_{0}-1}{l}\binom{n_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\leq\binom{k_{0}-1}{l}\binom{n_{0}}{k_{0}-1}.

∎

Now we finish the proof of Lemma
5.4
.

Lemma
5.4
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and any
z_{1},\cdots,z_{k}
on the unit circle of
\mathbb{C}
, there always exists a degree
m=O(k^{2}\log k)
polynomial
P(z)=\overset{m}{\underset{j=0}{\sum}}c_{j}z^{j}
with the following properties:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ I}

\displaystyle P(z_{i})=0,\forall i\in\{1,\cdots,k\},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ II}

\displaystyle c_{0}=1,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ III}

\displaystyle|c_{j}|\leq 11,\forall j\in\{1,\cdots,m\}.

Let
m=10k^{2}\log k
and
\mathcal{P}
denote a set of polynomials that has degree at most
m
, and all the coefficients are integers chosen from
\{-5,\cdots,-1,0,1,\cdots,5\}
, i.e.,

\mathcal{P}:=\left\{P(z)=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\alpha_{i}z^{i}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \forall i\in\{0,1,\cdots,m\},|\alpha_{i}|\leq 2\right\}.

Claim A.4
.

There exists
P^{*}(z)=\overset{m}{\underset{i=0}{\sum}}\alpha_{i}z^{i}
with coefficient
|\alpha_{i}|\leq 10
for any
i\in\{0,1,\cdots,m\}
, such that every coefficient of
P^{*}(z)\mod Q(z)
is bounded by
2^{-m}
.

Proof.

For
P(z)=\overset{m}{\underset{i=0}{\sum}}\alpha_{i}z^{i}\in\mathcal{P}
,
P(z)\mod Q(z)\equiv\overset{m}{\underset{i=0}{\sum}}\alpha_{i}r_{n,k}(z)
from the definition
r_{n,k}(z)
. Hence

P(z)\mod Q(z)=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\alpha_{i}\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}r_{i,k}^{(l)}z^{l}=\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}z^{l}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\alpha_{i}r_{i,k}^{(l)}.

Each coefficient in
P(z)\mod Q(z)
is bounded by
|\overset{m}{\underset{i=0}{\sum}}\alpha_{i}r_{i,k}^{(l)}|\leq 5\overset{m}{\underset{i=0}{\sum}}2^{k}i^{k-1}\leq 2^{k}m^{k}.

At the same time,
|\mathcal{P}|=11^{m}
. From the pigeonhole principle and
\left(\frac{2^{k}m^{k}}{(2^{-m})^{2}}\right)^{k}<11^{m}
, there exists
P_{1},P_{2}\in\mathcal{P}
such that for
P^{*}(z)=P_{1}(z)-P_{2}(z)
,
P^{*}(z)\mod Q(z)=\overset{k-1}{\underset{i=0}{\sum}}\gamma_{i}z^{i}
where each coefficient
|\gamma_{i}|\leq 2^{-m}
.
∎

Let
r(z)=\overset{k-1}{\underset{i=0}{\sum}}\gamma_{i}z^{i}=P^{*}(z)\mod Q(z)
for convenience. If
P^{*}(0)
(the constant term of
P^{*}
) is nonzero, then
|P^{*}(0)-r(0)|\geq 0.99
from the above lemma. Therefore the polynomial
\frac{P^{*}(z)-r(z)}{P^{*}(0)-r(0)}
satisfies the three properties in Lemma
5.4
.

Otherwise, we assume
z^{l}
is the first term in
P^{*}(z)
with a non-zero coefficient. Let

r_{-l,k}(z)=z^{-l}\mod Q(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\underset{j\in[k]\backslash i}{\prod}(z-z_{j})z_{i}^{-l}}{\underset{j\in[k]\backslash i}{\prod}(z_{i}-z_{j})}.

For convenience, we use
z_{S}=\underset{i\in S}{\prod}z_{i}
for any subset
S\subseteq[k]
. Notice that
z_{i}^{-l}=\frac{z^{l}_{[k]\setminus i}}{z_{[k]}^{l}}
. Hence
r_{-l,k}(z)=r^{\prime}_{l,k}(z)/z_{[k]}^{l}
where
r^{\prime}
is the polynomial for
k
units roots
z_{[k]\setminus 1},\cdots,z_{[k]\setminus k}
. So each coefficients of
r
is still bounded by
2^{k}l^{k}
, which is less than
2^{-m/2}
.

Eventually we choose
P^{*}(z)/z^{l}-r(z)\cdot r_{-l,k}(z)
and renormalize it to satisfy the three properties in Lemma
5.4
.

A.3
Proof of Lemma
4.3

Lemma
4.3
.

For any degree
d
polynomial
P(t):\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
with derivative
P^{\prime}(t)
, we have,

\int_{-1}^{1}(1-t^{2})|P^{\prime}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\leq 2d^{2}\int_{-1}^{1}|P(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

(28)

Given a degree
d
polynomial
P(x)
, we rewrite
P(x)
as a linear combination of the Legendre polynomials:

P(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}\alpha_{i}L_{i}(x).

We use
F_{i}(x)=(1-x^{2})L^{\prime}_{i}(x)
for convenience. From the definition of the Legendre polynomials in the Equation (
3
),
F^{\prime}_{i}(x)=-i(i+1)\cdot L_{i}(x)
and
F^{\prime\prime}_{i}(x)=-i(i+1)\cdot L^{\prime}_{i}(x)
.

Hence we have

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{-1}(1-x^{2})|P^{\prime}(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x=\quad

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{-1}(1-x^{2})P^{\prime}(x)\cdot\overline{P^{\prime}}(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{-1}\left(\sum_{i\in[d]}\alpha_{i}F_{i}(x)\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{i\in[d]}\overline{\alpha_{i}}\frac{-F^{\prime\prime}_{i}(x)}{i(i+1)}\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i\in[d]}\alpha_{i}F_{i}(x)\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{i\in[d]}\overline{\alpha_{i}}\frac{-F^{\prime}_{i}(x)}{i(i+1)}\right)\bigg|_{-1}^{1}

\displaystyle+\quad

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{-1}\left(\sum_{i\in[d]}\alpha_{i}F^{\prime}_{i}(x)\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{i\in[d]}\overline{\alpha_{i}}\frac{F^{\prime}_{i}(x)}{i(i+1)}\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{-1}\left(\sum_{i\in[d]}\alpha_{i}\cdot i(i+1)\cdot L_{i}(x)\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{i\in[d]}\overline{\alpha_{i}}\frac{i(i+1)\cdot L_{i}(x)}{i(i+1)}\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[d]}|\alpha_{i}|^{2}i(i+1)\|L_{i}\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\quad

\displaystyle d(d+1)\|P\|^{2}_{T}

A.4
Proof of Lemma
6.2

Lemma
6.2
.

\widehat{P_{\sigma,a,b}x}(\sigma(f-b))=\frac{1}{\sigma}e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\sigma af}\widehat{x}(f)
and
\widehat{P_{\sigma,a,b}x}(f)=\frac{1}{\sigma}e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\sigma a(f/\sigma+b)}\widehat{x}(f/\sigma+b)

Proof.

Let’s compute the Fourier Transform of
(P_{\sigma,a,b}x)(t)
,

\displaystyle\widehat{P_{\sigma,a,b}x}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}(P_{\sigma,a,b}(x))(t)e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}ft}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}x(\sigma(t-a))e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\sigma bt}e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}ft}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}(\sigma ab+fa)}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}x(\sigma(t-a))e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\sigma b(t-a)}e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}f(t-a)}\mathrm{d}t

by shifting
t
by
a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}(\sigma ab+fa)}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}x(\sigma t)e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\sigma bt}e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}ft}\mathrm{d}t

by replacing
t-a
by
t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sigma}e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}(\sigma ab+fa)}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}x(\sigma t)e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}b\sigma t}e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}f\sigma t/\sigma}\mathrm{d}\sigma t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sigma}e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}(\sigma ab+fa)}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}x(t)e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}(b+f/\sigma)t}\mathrm{d}t

by replacing
t\sigma
by
t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sigma}e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}a\sigma(f/\sigma+b)}\widehat{x}(f/\sigma+b)

by definition of FT

The first result follows immediately by replacing
f/\sigma+b
by
f^{\prime}
, which gives

\widehat{P_{\sigma,a,b}x}(\sigma(f^{\prime}-b))=\frac{1}{\sigma}e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}a\sigma f^{\prime}}\widehat{x}(f^{\prime}).

Thus, we complete the proof of this Lemma.
∎

A.5
Proof of Lemma
3.5

Lemma
3.5
.

Given any function
x(t):\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
with
\underset{t\in[0,T]}{\max}|x(t)|^{2}\leq d\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}
. Let
S
denote a set of points from
0
to
T
. If each point of
S
is chosen uniformly at random from
[0,T]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}\left[\left|\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i\in S}|x(t_{i})|^{2}-\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}]\right|\geq\epsilon\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{-\Omega(\epsilon^{2}|S|/d)}

Proof.

Let
M
denote
\underset{t\in[0,T]}{\max}|x(t)|^{2}
. Replacing
X_{i}
by
\frac{|x(t_{i})|^{2}}{M}
and
n
by
|S|
in Lemma
B.2
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}[|X-\mu|>\epsilon\mu]\leq 2\exp(-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{3}\mu)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}\left[\left|\sum_{i\in S}\frac{|x(t_{i})|^{2}}{M}-|S|\frac{\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}}{M}\right|>\epsilon|S|\frac{\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}}{M}\right]\leq 2\exp(-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{3}\mu)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}\left[\left|\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i\in S}|x(t_{i})|^{2}-\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}\right|\geq\epsilon\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}\right]\leq 2\exp(-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{3}\mu)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}\left[\left|\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i\in S}|x(t_{i})|^{2}-\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}\right|\geq\epsilon\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}\right]\leq 2\exp(-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{3}|S|\frac{\|x(t)\|_{T}^{2}}{M})

which is less than
2\exp(-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{3}|S|/d)
, thus completes the proof.
∎

A.6
Proof of Lemma
3.10

Lemma
3.10
.

For any polynomial
P(t)
of degree at most
d
from
R
to
\mathbb{C}
, for any interval
[S,T]
,

\max_{t\in[S,T]}|P(t)|^{2}\leq(d+1)^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{T-S}\int_{S}^{T}|P(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x.

Proof.

Let
t^{*}=\underset{t\in[S,T]}{\arg\max}|P(t)|^{2}
. If
t^{*}\in(S,T)
, then it is enough to prove that

|P(t^{*})|^{2}\leq(d+1)^{2}\frac{1}{t^{*}-S}\int_{S}^{t^{*}}|P(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\text{ and }|P(t^{*})|^{2}\leq(d+1)^{2}\frac{1}{T-t^{*}}\int_{t^{*}}^{T}|P(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x

on the two intervals
[S,t^{*}]
and
[t^{*},T]
separately.

Without loss of generality, we will prove the inequality for
S=-1
and
t^{*}=T=1
. We find the minimum
\|P(x)\|^{2}_{T}
assuming
|P(1)|^{2}=1
. Because the first
(d+1)
Legendre polynomials provide a basis of polynomials of degree at most
d
and their evaluation
L_{n}(1)=1
for any
n
, we consider:

\displaystyle\underset{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{d}\in\mathbb{C}}{\min}

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}|P(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x

s.t.

\displaystyle P(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}\alpha_{i}L_{i}(x)

\displaystyle|P(1)|=|\sum_{i=0}^{d}\alpha_{i}|=1.

We simplify the integration of
P(x)^{2}
over
[-1,1]
by the orthogonality of Legendre polynomials:

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}|P(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d}\alpha_{i}L_{i}(x)\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{j=0}^{d}\overline{\alpha_{j}}\overline{L_{j}}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\sum_{i=0}^{d}|\alpha_{i}|^{2}L_{i}(x)^{2}+\sum_{i\neq j}\alpha_{i}\overline{\alpha_{j}}L_{i}(x)\overline{L_{j}}(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{d}|\alpha_{i}|^{2}\frac{2}{2i+1}\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ Fact\penalty\ \ref{fac:legendre_inner_product}}

Using
\int_{-1}^{1}|P(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x=\overset{d}{\underset{i=0}{\sum}}|\alpha_{i}|^{2}\frac{2}{2i+1}
, we simplify the optimization problem to

\displaystyle\underset{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{d}\in\C}{\min}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{d}|\alpha_{i}|^{2}\frac{2}{2i+1}

s.t.

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i=0}^{d}\alpha_{i}\right|=1

From the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

\left|\sum_{i=0}^{d}\alpha_{i}\right|^{2}\leq\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d}|\alpha_{i}|^{2}\frac{2}{2i+1}\right)\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d}\frac{2i+1}{2}\right).

Therefore
\overset{d}{\underset{i=0}{\sum}}|\alpha_{i}|^{2}\frac{2}{2i+1}\geq\frac{2}{(d+1)^{2}}
and
|P(1)|^{2}\leq(d+1)^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}|P(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x
.
∎

Appendix B
Known Facts

This section provides a list of well-known facts existing in literature.

B.1
Inequalities

We state the Hölder’s inequality for complex numbers. We will use the corresponding version
p=q=2
of Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for complex numbers.

Lemma B.1

(Hölder’s inequality)
.

If
S
is a measurable subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with the Lebesgue measure, and
f
and
g
are measurable complex-valued functions on
S
, then

\int_{S}|f(x)g(x)|\mathrm{d}x\leq(\int_{S}|f(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x)^{\frac{1}{p}}(\int_{S}|g(x)|^{q}\mathrm{d}x)^{\frac{1}{q}}

Lemma B.2

(Chernoff Bound
[
Tar09
]
,
[
Che52
]
)
.

Let
X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{n}
be independent random variables. Assume that
0\leq X_{i}\leq 1
always, for each
i\in[n]
. Let
X=X_{1}+X_{2}+\cdots+X_{n}
and
\mu=\mathbb{E}[X]=\overset{n}{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}\mathbb{E}[X_{i}]
. Then for any
\epsilon>0
,

\mathsf{Pr}[X\geq(1+\epsilon)\mu]\leq\exp(-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2+\epsilon}\mu)\textit{ and }\mathsf{Pr}[X\geq(1-\epsilon)\mu]\leq\exp(-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}\mu).

B.2
Linear regression

Given a linear subspace
\mathrm{span}\{\vec{v}_{1},\cdots,\vec{v}_{d}\}
and
n
points, we always use
\ell_{2}
-regression to find a vector as the linear combination of
\vec{v}_{1},\cdots,\vec{v}_{d}
that minimizes the distance of this vector to those
n
points.

Fact B.3
.

Given an
n\times d
matrix
A
and an
n\times 1
column vector
b
, it takes
O(nd^{\omega-1})
time to output an
x^{\prime}
such that

x^{\prime}=\underset{x}{\arg\min}\|Ax-b\|_{2}.

where
\omega
is the exponent of matrix multiplication
[
Wil12
]
.

Notice that weighted linear regression can be solved by linear regression solver as a black-box.

Algorithm 1

Linear regression algorithms

1:

procedure

LinearRegression
(
A,b,
) — Fact
B.3

2:

x^{\prime}\leftarrow\underset{x}{\arg\min}\|Ax-b\|_{2}
.

3:

return

x^{\prime}

4:

end

procedure

5:

procedure

LinearRegressionW
(
A,b,w
) — Fact
B.3

6:

x^{\prime}\leftarrow\underset{x}{\arg\min}\overset{d}{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}w_{i}|(Ax)_{i}-b_{i}|^{2}
.

7:

return

x^{\prime}

8:

end

procedure

Algorithm 2

Multipoint evaluation of a polynomial

1:

procedure

MultipointEvaluation
(
P,\{t_{1},t_{2},\cdots,t_{d}\}
) — Fact
B.4

2:

return

P(t_{1}),P(t_{2}),\cdots,P(t_{d})

3:

end

procedure

B.3
Multipoint evaluation of a polynomial

Given a degree-
d
polynomial, and
n
locations. The naive algorithm of computing the evaluations at those
n
locations takes
O(nd)
. However, the running time can be improved to
O(n\poly(\log d))
by using this well-known result,

Fact B.4

(
[
BS12
]
)
.

Given a degree-
d
polynomial
P(t)
, and a set of
d
locations
\{t_{1},t_{2},\cdots,t_{d}\}
. There exists an algorithm that takes
O(d\log^{c}d)
time to output the evaluations
\{P(t_{1}),P(t_{2}),\cdots,P(t_{d})\}
, for some constant
c
.

Appendix C
Analysis of Hash Functions and Filter Functions

C.1
Analysis of filter function
\texorpdfstring
(H(t),\widehat{H}(f))

We construct the Filter function
(H(t),\widehat{H}(f))
in this section.

We fix the interval to be
\supp(\rect_{1})=[-1/2,1/2]
instead of
[0,T]
for convenience. We first define the filter function
H_{1}(t)
which preserves the energy of a
k
-Fourier-sparse signal
x^{*}
on
[-1/2,1/2]
to the signal
H_{1}\cdot x^{*}
on
[-\infty,+\infty]
.

Definition C.1
.

Let
s_{1}=\Theta(k^{4}\log^{4}k)
,
\ell=\Omega(k\log k/\delta)
be a even number, and
s_{0}=C_{0}s_{1}\sqrt{\ell}
for some constant
C_{0}
that will normalize
H_{1}(0)=1
. Recall that
\rect_{s}(t)=1
iff
|t|\leq s/2
and
\widehat{\rect_{s}(f)}=\sinc(fs)=\frac{\sin(\pi fs)}{\pi fs}
.

We define the filter function
H_{1}(t)
and its Fourier transform
\widehat{H}_{1}(f)
as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{H}_{1}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{0}\cdot\left(\rect_{s_{1}}(f)\right)^{*\ell}\cdot\sinc\left(fs_{2}\right),

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{0}\cdot\bigl({\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[width]{rect_s1}}}}\bigr)^{*\ell}\cdot{\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[width]{sinc_s2}}}}

\displaystyle H_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{0}\cdot\left(\sinc(s_{1}t)\right)^{\cdot\ell}*\rect_{s_{2}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{0}\cdot\bigl({\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[width]{sinc_s1}}}}\bigr)^{\cdot\ell}*{\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[width]{rect_s2}}}}

where
s_{0}
is a fixed parameter s.t.
H_{1}(0)=1
.

We provide some basic properties about our filter function. Notice that
\sinc(t)=\frac{\sin(\pi t)}{\pi t}
(
\sinc(0)
is defined to be
1
) has the following properties (shown in Figure
4
):

1.

\forall t\in\mathbb{R},1-\frac{(\pi t)^{2}}{3!}\leq\sinc(t)\leq 1
.

2.

\forall|t|\leq 1.2/\pi,\sinc(t)\leq 1-\frac{t^{2}}{8}
.

3.

\forall|t|>1.2/\pi,|\sinc(t)|\leq\frac{1}{\pi|t|}
.

Figure 4:
The Property of
\sinc(t)
.

Claim C.2
.

\int_{-\frac{1.2}{\pi s_{1}}}^{\frac{1.2}{\pi s_{1}}}(\sinc(s_{1}t))^{\ell}\mathrm{d}t\eqsim\frac{1}{s_{1}\sqrt{\ell}}
.

Proof.

We use the above properties for the sinc function to prove the upper bound:

\displaystyle\int_{-\frac{1.2}{\pi s_{1}}}^{+\frac{1.2}{\pi s_{1}}}(\sinc(s_{1}t))^{\ell}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{s_{1}}\int_{-1.2/\pi}^{+1.2/\pi}(\sinc(t))^{\ell}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{s_{1}}\left(\int_{0}^{\sqrt{8/\ell}}(\sinc{t})^{\ell}\mathrm{d}t+\int_{\sqrt{8/\ell}}^{1.2/\pi}(\sinc(t))^{\ell}\mathrm{d}t\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{s_{1}}\left(\sqrt{8/\ell}+\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{1.2/\pi}{\sqrt{8/\ell}}-1}\int_{i\sqrt{8/\ell}}^{(i+1)\sqrt{8/\ell}}(1-x^{2}/8)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}x\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{s_{1}}\left(\sqrt{8/\ell}+\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{1.2/\pi}{\sqrt{8/\ell}}-1}\sqrt{8/\ell}\cdot 2^{-i^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{s_{1}\sqrt{\ell}}.

We prove the lower bound:

\displaystyle\int_{-\frac{1.2}{\pi s_{1}}}^{\frac{1.2}{\pi s_{1}}}(\sinc(s_{1}t))^{\ell}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{s_{1}}\left(\int_{0}^{\sqrt{8/\ell}}(\sinc{t})^{\ell}\mathrm{d}t+\int_{\sqrt{8/\ell}}^{1.2/\pi}(\sinc(t))^{\ell}\mathrm{d}t\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2}{s_{1}}\left(\int_{0}^{\sqrt{8/\ell}}(1-\frac{\pi^{2}t^{2}}{6})^{\ell}\mathrm{d}t\right)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{1}{s_{1}\sqrt{\ell}}.

∎

We bound the integration outside
[-\frac{1.2}{\pi s_{1}},\frac{1.2}{\pi s_{1}}]
from the last property of the sinc function.

Claim C.3
.

\int_{\frac{1.2}{\pi s_{1}}}^{+\infty}(\sinc(s_{1}t))^{\ell}\mathrm{d}t=O(1.2^{-\ell})
.

From these two claims, we have the existence of
s_{0}
.

Claim C.4
.

There exists a universal constant
C_{0}
and
s_{0}=C_{0}s_{1}\sqrt{\ell}
such that
H_{1}(0)=1
.

Proof.

Because
\ell
is a large even number,
\int_{\rect_{1-2/s_{1}}}\sinc(s_{1}t)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}t\eqsim\frac{1}{s_{1}\sqrt{\ell}}
from all discussion above.
∎

We show several useful properties about the Filter functions
\big(H_{1}(t),\widehat{H_{1}}(f)\big)
.

Lemma C.5
.

Given
s_{0},s_{1},s_{2},\ell
, where
\frac{s_{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{s_{1}}\leq 1/2
and
s_{0}=C_{0}s_{1}\sqrt{\ell}
for some constant
C_{0}
. The filter function
(H_{1}(t),\widehat{H}_{1}(f))[s_{0},s_{1},s_{2},\ell]
has the following properties,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ I}:

\displaystyle H_{1}(t)\in[1-\frac{s_{0}}{s_{1}}\cdot\frac{2\pi^{-\ell}}{\ell-1},1],\text{\penalty\ if\penalty\ }|t|\leq\frac{s_{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{s_{1}}.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ II}:

\displaystyle H_{1}(t)\in[0,1],\text{\penalty\ if\penalty\ }\frac{s_{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{s_{1}}\leq|t|\leq\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ III}:

\displaystyle H_{1}(t)\leq s_{0}s_{2}\left((s_{1}|t|-s_{1}+2)^{2}+1\right)^{-\ell},\forall|t|>\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ IV}:

\displaystyle\supp(\widehat{H}_{1}(f))\subseteq[-\frac{s_{1}\ell}{2},\frac{s_{1}\ell}{2}]

Figure 5:
The light red area represents
\int_{(1/2-2/s_{1})}^{(1/2-2/s_{1})-t}s_{0}\cdot\sinc\left(s_{1}(\tau)\right)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau
and the light green area represents
\int_{1/2-2/s_{1}}^{1/2-2/s_{1}+t}s_{0}\cdot\sinc\left(s_{1}(\tau)\right)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau
.

Proof of Property I.

First,
H_{1}(0)=1
follows by definition of
s_{0}
,
then we can prove the upper bound for
H_{1}(t)
by showing for any
t>0
,
H_{1}(0)-H_{1}(t)>0
always holds ,

By definition of sinc function, we know that
\sinc(s_{1}t)^{\ell}
reaches
0
at all the points
\{\frac{1}{s_{1}}+i\frac{2}{s_{1}}|i\in\mathbb{N}\}
. By definition of
s_{1}
, we know that
\frac{1}{s_{1}}\ll\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s_{1}}
. For any
t>0
,

\displaystyle H_{1}(0)-H_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\int s_{0}\cdot\sinc\left(s_{1}(\tau)\right)^{\ell}\cdot\rect_{1-2/s_{1}}(0-\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau-\int s_{0}\cdot\sinc\left(s_{1}(\tau)\right)^{\ell}\cdot\rect_{1-2/s_{1}}(t-\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\int_{-(1/2-2/s_{1})}^{1/2-2/s_{1}}s_{0}\cdot\sinc\left(s_{1}(\tau)\right)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau-\int_{-(1/2-2/s_{1})+t}^{1/2-2/s_{1}+t}s_{0}\cdot\sinc\left(s_{1}(\tau)\right)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\int_{-(1/2-2/s_{1})}^{-(1/2-2/s_{1})+t}s_{0}\cdot\sinc\left(s_{1}(\tau)\right)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau-\int_{1/2-2/s_{1}}^{1/2-2/s_{1}+t}s_{0}\cdot\sinc\left(s_{1}(\tau)\right)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau

shown in Figure
5

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle 0,

where the last inequality follows by choosing
s_{1}
to be an integer. Thus, we prove an upper bound for
H_{1}(t)
.
Third, we show the lower bound for
H_{1}(t)
,

\displaystyle H_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}s_{0}\cdot\sinc(s_{1}\tau)^{\cdot\ell}\rect_{s_{2}}(t-\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{t-\frac{s_{2}}{2}}^{t+\frac{s_{2}}{2}}s_{0}\cdot\sinc(s_{1}\tau)^{\cdot\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\underbrace{\int_{t+\frac{s_{2}}{2}}^{+\infty}s_{0}\cdot\sinc(s_{1}\tau)^{\cdot\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau}_{A}-\underbrace{\int_{-\infty}^{t-\frac{s_{2}}{2}}s_{0}\cdot\sinc(s_{1}\tau)^{\cdot\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau}_{B}

Thus, as long as we can upper bound the term
A
and
B
, then we will have a lower bound for the
H_{1}(t)
, for any
|t|\leq\frac{s_{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{s_{1}}
.

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{t+\frac{s_{2}}{2}}^{+\infty}s_{0}\cdot\sinc(s_{1}\tau)^{\cdot\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{1}{s_{1}}}^{+\infty}s_{0}\cdot\sinc(s_{1}\tau)^{\cdot\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{1}{s_{1}}}^{+\infty}s_{0}\cdot(s_{1}\pi\tau)^{-\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{0}\cdot(s_{1}\pi)^{-\ell}\frac{1}{\ell-1}(1/s_{1})^{-\ell+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{s_{0}}{s_{1}}\cdot(\pi)^{-\ell}\frac{1}{\ell-1}

Similarly, we can bound the term
B
in the same way.
∎

Proof of Property II.

In the proof of Property I, we already show that
\forall t
,
H_{1}(t)\leq 1
. Thus, the upper bound of Property II is also holding. The lower bound follows by both
\sinc(s_{1}t)^{\cdot\ell}
and
\rect_{s_{2}}(t)
are always nonnegative, thus the convolution of these two functions has to be nonnegative.
∎

Proof of Property III.

Let’s prove the case when
t>1
, since
H_{1}(t)
is symmetric, then the case
t<-1
will also hold. By definition of
(H_{1}(t),\widehat{H}_{1}(f))
, we have

\displaystyle H_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{0}\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\sinc(s_{1}(t-\tau))^{\cdot\ell}\rect_{s_{2}}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{0}\cdot\int_{-\frac{s_{2}}{2}}^{\frac{s_{2}}{2}}\sinc(s_{1}(t-\tau))^{\cdot\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{0}\cdot\int_{-\frac{s_{2}}{2}}^{\frac{s_{2}}{2}}\sinc(s_{1}(\tau-t))^{\cdot\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau

We’d like to choose a middle point
\tau_{0}
, and then separated the interval into two parts, one is
[-\frac{s_{2}}{2},\tau_{0}]
and the other is
[\tau_{0},\frac{s_{2}}{2}]
. To choose a reasonable
\tau_{0}
, we need to use the following simple facts,

\displaystyle(\frac{\sin(x)}{x})^{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle x^{-\ell}\text{\penalty\ if\penalty\ }x\geq 1.2

\displaystyle(\frac{\sin(x)}{x})^{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1-\frac{x^{2}}{8})^{\ell}\text{\penalty\ if\penalty\ }x<1.2

Thus,
|\pi s_{1}(\tau_{0}-t)|=1.2
, which implies that
\tau_{0}^{+}=t+\frac{1.2}{\pi s_{1}}
or
\tau_{0}^{-}=t-\frac{1.2}{\pi s_{1}}
. By relationship between
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
, we know
\tau_{0}^{-}>\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1.2}{\pi s_{1}}>\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s_{1}}\geq\frac{s_{2}}{2}
. Thus, we can use the case
x<1.2
to upper bound the
H_{1}(t)
,

\displaystyle\penalty\ H_{1}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle s_{0}\cdot s_{2}\cdot\underset{\tau\in[-s_{2}/2,s_{2}/2]}{\max}\sinc(s_{1}(\tau-t))^{\cdot\ell}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle s_{0}\cdot s_{2}\underset{\tau\in[-s_{2}/2,s_{2}/2]}{\max}(1-\frac{(s_{1}\pi(\tau-t))^{2}}{8})^{\ell}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle s_{0}\cdot s_{2}(1-\frac{(s_{1}\pi(\frac{s_{2}}{2}-t))^{2}}{8})^{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle s_{0}\cdot s_{2}\cdot(e^{-\frac{(s_{1}\pi(\frac{s_{2}}{2}-t))^{2}}{8}})^{\ell}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }1-x\leq e^{-x}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle s_{0}\cdot s_{2}\cdot(e^{(s_{1}(t-s_{2}/2))^{2}})^{-\ell}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }1<\pi^{2}/8

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle s_{0}\cdot s_{2}\cdot(1+(s_{1}(t-s_{2}/2))^{2})^{-\ell}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }1+x\leq e^{x}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle s_{0}\cdot(1+(s_{1}(t-s_{2}/2))^{2})^{-\ell}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }s_{1}\leq 1.

Thus, we complete the proof.
∎

Proof of Property IV.
Because of the support of
\rect_{s_{1}}(f)
is
s_{1}
, then the support of
(\rect_{s_{1}}(f))^{*\ell}=s_{1}\ell
. Since
\widehat{H}_{1}(f)
is defined to be the
(\rect_{s_{1}}(f))^{*\ell}
multiplied by
\sinc(fs_{2})
, thus
\supp(\widehat{H}_{1}(f))\subseteq[-\frac{s_{1}\ell}{2},\frac{s_{1}\ell}{2}]
.
∎

Definition C.6
.

Given any
0<s_{3}<1
,
0<\delta<1
, we define
(H(t),\widehat{H}(f))
to be the filter function
(H_{1}(t),\widehat{H_{1}}(f))
by doing the following operations

•

Setting
\ell=\Theta(k\log(k/\delta))
,

•

Setting
s_{2}=1-\frac{2}{s_{1}}
,

•

Shrinking by a factor
s_{3}
in time domain,

\displaystyle H(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{1}(t/s_{3})

(29)

\displaystyle\widehat{H}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{3}\widehat{H_{1}}(s_{3}f)

(30)

We call the “heavy cluster" around a frequency
f_{0}
to be the support of
\delta_{f_{0}}(f)*\widehat{H}(f)
in the frequency domain and use

\Delta_{h}=|\supp(\widehat{H}(f))|=\frac{s_{1}\cdot\ell}{s_{3}}

(31)

to denote the width of the cluster.

We show several useful properties about the Filter functions
\big(H(t),\widehat{H}(f)\big)
.

Lemma
6.6
.

Given
s_{0},s_{1},0<s_{3}<1,\ell>1,0<\delta<1
, where
\ell=\Theta(k\log(k/\delta))
.The filter function
(H(t),\widehat{H}(f))
has the following properties,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ I}:

\displaystyle H(t)\in[1-\delta,1],\text{\penalty\ when\penalty\ }|t|\leq(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{s_{1}})s_{3}.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ II}:

\displaystyle H(t)\in[0,1],\text{\penalty\ when\penalty\ }(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{s_{1}})s_{3}\leq|t|\leq\frac{1}{2}s_{3}.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ III}:

\displaystyle H(t)\leq s_{0}\cdot(s_{1}(\frac{|t|}{s_{3}}-\frac{1}{2})+2)^{-\ell},\forall|t|>\frac{1}{2}s_{3}.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ IV}:

\displaystyle\supp(\widehat{H}(f))\subseteq[-\frac{s_{1}\ell}{2s_{3}},\frac{s_{1}\ell}{2s_{3}}].

For any exact
k
-Fourier-sparse signal
x^{*}(t)
, we shift the interval from
[0,T]
to
[-1/2,1/2]
and consider
x^{*}(t)
for
t\in[-1/2,1/2]
to be our observation, which is also
x^{*}(t)\cdot\rect_{1}(t)
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ V}:

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\bigl|x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t)\cdot(1-\rect_{1}(t))\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}t<\delta\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|x^{*}(t)\cdot\rect_{1}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ VI}:

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t)\cdot\rect_{1}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\in[1-\epsilon,1]\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|x^{*}(t)\cdot\rect_{1}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

for arbitrarily small constant
\epsilon
.

The Property I, II, III and IV follow by filter function
H(t),\widehat{H}(f)
inheriting
H_{1}(t),\widehat{H}_{1}(f)
.

Figure 6:
\widehat{H\cdot x^{*}}(f)
and
H\cdot x^{*}(t)
.

Proof of Property V.

\forall t\notin[-1/2,1/2]
, we have,

\displaystyle|x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\cdot|H(t)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\cdot(s_{1}(|t|-1/2)+2)^{-\ell}

by Property III of
H_{1}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle k^{7}\cdot(2kt)^{2.5k}\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|x^{*}(t)\cdot\rect_{1}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\cdot(s_{1}(\frac{|t|}{s_{3}}-\frac{1}{2})+2)^{-\ell}\penalty\

by Lemma
5.5

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle t^{O(k\log k)}\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|x^{*}(t)\cdot\rect_{1}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\cdot(s_{1}(\frac{|t|}{s_{3}}-\frac{1}{2})+2)^{-\ell/2}.

(32)

Thus taking the integral finishes the proof because
\ell\gtrsim k\log(k/\delta)
.
∎

Proof of Property VI.

First, because of for any
t
,
|H_{1}(t)|\leq 1
, thus we prove the upper bound for
LHS
,

\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t)\cdot\rect_{1}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|x^{*}(t)\cdot 1\cdot\rect_{1}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

Second, as mentioned early, we need to prove the general case when
s_{3}=1-1/\poly(k)
. Define interval
S=[-s_{3}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s_{1}}),s_{3}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s_{1}})]
, by definition,
S\subset[-1/2,1/2]
. Then define
\overline{S}=[-1/2,1/2]\setminus S
, which is
[-1/2,-s_{3}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s_{1}}))\cup(s_{3}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s_{1}}),1/2]
. By Property I, we have

\int_{S}|x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\geq(1-\delta)^{2}\int_{S}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

(33)

Then we can show

\displaystyle\int_{\overline{S}}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle|\overline{S}|\cdot\underset{t\in[-1/2,1/2]}{\max}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle(1-s_{3}(1-\frac{2}{s_{1}}))\cdot\widetilde{O}(k^{4})\int_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

by Lemma
5.1

\displaystyle\lesssim\penalty\

\displaystyle\int_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }\min(\frac{1}{1-s_{3}},s_{1})\geq\widetilde{O}(k^{4})

(34)

Combining Equations (
33
) and (
C.1
) gives a lower bound for
LHS
,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t)\cdot\rect_{1}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle\int_{S}|x^{*}(t)H(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle(1-2\delta)\int_{S}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

by Equation (
33
)

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle(1-2\delta)\int_{S\cup\overline{S}}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t-(1-2\delta)\int_{\overline{S}}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle(1-2\delta)\int_{S\cup\overline{S}}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t-(1-2\delta)\epsilon\int_{S\cup\overline{S}}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

by Equation (
C.1
)

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle(1-2\delta-\epsilon)\int_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle(1-2\epsilon)\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|x^{*}(t)\cdot\rect_{1}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }\epsilon\gg\delta

∎

Figure 7:
Property VI of filter function
H(t)
, the light green area represents
\mathrm{RHS}
(without scalar) of Property VI of filter
H
, the light red area represents
\mathrm{LHS}
of Property VI of filter
H
, the light yellow area represents the difference. Property VI says the light yellow area is only a small constant fraction of the light green area.

Remark C.7
.

To match
(H(t),\widehat{H}(f))
on
[-1/2,1/2]
with signal
x(t)
on
[0,T]
, we will scale the time domain from
[-1/2,1/2]
to
[-T/2,T/2]
and shift it to
[0,T]
. For example, the rectangle function in Property V and VI will be replaced by
\rect_{T}(t-T/2)
. For the parameters
s_{0},s_{1},s_{3},\delta,\ell
in the definition of
H
, we always treat them as numbers. We assume
T
has seconds as unit and
\Delta_{h}
has Hz as unit . For example, in time domain, the Property I becomes that given
T>0
,

H(t)\in[1-\delta,1]\text{\penalty\ if\penalty\ }|t-\frac{T}{2}|\leq(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s_{1}})s_{3}\cdot T

In frequency domain, the Property IV becomes

\supp(\widehat{H}(f))\subseteq[-\frac{\Delta_{h}}{2},\frac{\Delta_{h}}{2}],\text{\penalty\ where\penalty\ }\Delta_{h}=\frac{s_{1}\ell}{s_{3}T}.

(35)

Lemma C.8
.

Let
H(t)
denote the function defined in Definition
C.6
. For any
t\in[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
, there exists an algorithm that takes
O(s_{1}+\ell\log(s_{1})+\log(1/\epsilon))
time to output a value
\widetilde{H}(t)
such that

(1-\epsilon)H(t)\leq\widetilde{H}(t)\leq(1+\epsilon)H(t).

Proof.

We will show that using a low degree polynomial with sufficiently large degree is able to approximate the sinc function.
By definition of filter function,

\displaystyle H(t)

\displaystyle=s_{0}\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\sinc(s_{1}\tau)^{\cdot\ell}\rect_{s_{2}}(t-\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=s_{0}\cdot\int_{t-\frac{s_{2}}{2}}^{t+\frac{s_{2}}{2}}(\frac{\sin(\pi s_{1}\tau)}{\pi s_{1}\tau})^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{s_{0}}{\pi s_{1}}\int_{(t-\frac{s_{2}}{2})\pi s_{1}}^{(t+\frac{s_{2}}{2})\pi s_{1}}(\frac{\sin(\tau)}{\tau})^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{s_{0}}{\pi s_{1}}\int_{(t-\frac{s_{2}}{2})\pi s_{1}}^{(t+\frac{s_{2}}{2})\pi s_{1}}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{i}\frac{\tau^{2i}}{(2i+1)!}\right)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau

by Taylor expansion

\displaystyle=\frac{s_{0}}{\pi s_{1}}\int_{(t-\frac{s_{2}}{2})\pi s_{1}}^{(t+\frac{s_{2}}{2})\pi s_{1}}(A+B)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau

where the last step follows by setting
A=\sum_{i=0}^{d}(-1)^{i}\frac{\tau^{2i}}{(2i+1)!}
, and
B=\sum_{i=d+1}^{\infty}(-1)^{i}\frac{\tau^{2i}}{(2i+1)!}
.

Denote
I^{+}=(t+\frac{s_{2}}{2})\pi s_{1}
and
I^{-}=(t-\frac{s_{2}}{2})\pi s_{1}
. Because of
t\in[-1/2,1/2]
, then
\max(|I^{+}|,|I^{-}|)=O(s_{1})
. The goal is to show that

(1-\epsilon)\int_{I^{-}}^{I^{+}}(A+B)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau\leq\int_{I^{-}}^{I^{+}}A^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau\leq(1+\epsilon)\int_{I^{-}}^{I^{+}}(A+B)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau

Let’s prove an upper first,

\displaystyle\int_{I^{-}}^{I^{+}}(A+B-B)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{I^{-}}^{I^{+}}(A+B)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\int_{I^{-}}^{I^{+}}{\ell\choose j}(A+B)^{\ell-j}(-B)^{j}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{I^{-}}^{I^{+}}(A+B)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\int_{I^{-}}^{I^{+}}{\ell\choose j}|A+B|^{\ell-j}|B|^{j}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{I^{-}}^{I^{+}}(A+B)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\int_{I^{-}}^{I^{+}}{\ell\choose j}|A+B|^{\ell-j}\mathrm{d}\tau\cdot\max_{\tau\in[I^{-},I^{+}]}|B|^{j}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{I^{-}}^{I^{+}}(A+B)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau+\ell 2^{\ell}\cdot\max_{\tau\in[I^{-},I^{+}]}|B|

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }|H(t)|\leq 1\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }|B|^{j}\leq|B|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{I^{-}}^{I^{+}}(A+B)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau+\epsilon\cdot(s_{1})^{-\Theta(\ell)}

by Claim
C.9

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1+\epsilon)\int_{I^{-}}^{I^{+}}(A+B)^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau

by Claim
C.10

where all the steps by setting
d\gtrsim s_{1}+\ell\log(s_{1})+\log(1/\epsilon)
. Similarly, we can prove a lower bound.
∎

Claim C.9
.

Let
B(\tau)=\sum_{i=d+1}^{+\infty}(-1)\frac{\tau^{2i}}{(2i+1)!}
, if
d\gtrsim\tau+\ell\log(s_{1})+\log(1/\epsilon)
then
|B(\tau)|\leq\epsilon(1/s_{1})^{O(\ell)}
.

Proof.

We first show, for any
i\geq d+1
,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau^{2i}}{(2i+1)!}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\tau^{2i}}{e((2i+1)/e)^{2i+1}}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }e(n/e)^{n}\leq n!

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle 2^{-2i}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }i\gtrsim\tau

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\epsilon(1/s_{1})^{O(\ell)}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }i\gtrsim\ell\log(s_{1})+\log(1/\epsilon)

Second, we can show that

\sum_{i=d+1}^{+\infty}(-1)\frac{\tau^{2i}}{(2i+1)!}\lesssim\frac{\tau^{2(d+1)}}{(2(d+1)+1)!}\leq\epsilon(1/s_{1})^{O(\ell)}

Thus, we complete the proof.
∎

Claim C.10
.

\min_{t\in[-1/2,1/2]}|H(t)|\geq(s_{1})^{-\Omega(\ell)}
.

Proof.

By the property of
H(t)
,

\min_{\frac{1}{2}s_{3}<|t|\leq\frac{1}{2}}H(t)=\min_{|t|\leq\frac{1}{2}}H(t)

Thus, it suffices to prove a lower bound on
H(t)
for any
t
such that
\frac{1}{2}s_{3}<|t|\leq\frac{1}{2}
.
Because of symmetric property, we only need to prove a lower bound for one side.
Let’s consider
t\in[\frac{1}{2}s_{3},1/2]
,

\displaystyle H(t)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \geq\frac{s_{0}}{\pi s_{1}}\int_{(t-\frac{s_{2}}{2})\pi s_{1}}^{(t+\frac{s_{2}}{2})\pi s_{1}}(\frac{\sin(\tau)}{\tau})^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\penalty\ \geq\frac{s_{0}}{\pi s_{1}}\int_{(t+\frac{s_{2}}{4})\pi s_{1}}^{(t+\frac{s_{2}}{2})\pi s_{1}}(\frac{\sin(\tau)}{\tau})^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\penalty\ \geq\frac{s_{0}}{\pi s_{1}}\cdot\Theta((t+\frac{s_{2}}{2})s_{1})\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot\pi\cdot\Theta((t+\frac{s_{2}}{2})\pi s_{1})^{-\ell}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \geq(s_{1})^{-\Omega(\ell)}

∎

C.2
Analysis of filter function
\texorpdfstring
(G(t),\widehat{G}(f))

We construct
(G(t),\widehat{G}(f))
in a similar way of
(H_{1}(t),\widehat{H_{1}}(f))
by switching the time domain and the frequency domain of
(H_{1}(t),\widehat{H_{1}}(f))
and modify the parameters for the permutation hashing
P_{\sigma,a,b}
.

Definition C.11
.

Given
B>1
,
\delta>0
,
\alpha>0
, we construct
G(t),\widehat{G}(f)
by doing the following operations,

•

s_{2}=\frac{\pi}{2B}
,

•

s_{1}=\frac{B}{\alpha\pi}
,

•

\ell=l=\Theta(\log(k/\delta))
.

Then
G(t),\widehat{G}(f)
becomes

\displaystyle G(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b_{0}\cdot(\rect_{s_{1}}(t))^{*l}\cdot\sinc(ts_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b_{0}\cdot(\rect_{\frac{B}{(\alpha\pi)}}(t))^{*l}\cdot\sinc(t\frac{\pi}{2B}),

\displaystyle\widehat{G}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b_{0}\cdot(\sinc(s_{1}f))^{\cdot l}*\rect_{s_{2}}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b_{0}\cdot(\sinc(\frac{B}{\alpha\pi}f))^{\cdot l}*\rect_{\frac{\pi}{2B}}(f).

where the scalar
b_{0}=\Theta(s_{1}\sqrt{l})=\Theta(B\sqrt{l}/\alpha)
satisfying
\widehat{G}(0)=1
.

Lemma
6.7
.

Given
B>1
,
\delta>0
,
\alpha>0
, we set
l=\Omega(\log(\delta/k))
. The filter function
(G(t),\widehat{G}(f))[B,\delta,\alpha,l]
satisfies the following properties,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ I}:

\displaystyle\widehat{G}(f)\in[1-\delta/k,1],\text{\penalty\ if\penalty\ }|f|\leq(1-\alpha)\frac{2\pi}{2B}.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ II}:

\displaystyle\widehat{G}(f)\in[0,1],\text{\penalty\ if\penalty\ }(1-\alpha)\frac{2\pi}{2B}\leq|f|\leq\frac{2\pi}{2B}.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ III}:

\displaystyle\widehat{G}(f)\in[-\delta/k,\delta/k],\text{\penalty\ if\penalty\ }|f|>\frac{2\pi}{2B}.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ IV}:

\displaystyle\supp(G(t))\subset[\frac{l}{2}\cdot\frac{-B}{\pi\alpha},\frac{l}{2}\cdot\frac{B}{\pi\alpha}].

\displaystyle\mathrm{Property\penalty\ V}:

\displaystyle\underset{t}{\max}|G(t)|\lesssim\poly(B,l).

Proof.

The first five Properties follows from Lemma
6.6
directly.

∎

C.3
Parameters setting for filters

One-cluster Recovery.

Parameters for
s_{1},s_{3}
and
\ell
.

In one-cluster, we donot need filter function
(G(t),\widehat{G}(f))
.

In section
C.1
, by Equation (
C.1
) in the proof of Property VI of filter function
(H(t),\widehat{H}(f))
, we need
\min(\frac{1}{1-s_{3}},s_{1})\geq\widetilde{O}(k^{4})
.

In section
C.1
, by Equation (
C.1
) in the proof of Property V of filter function
(H(t),\widehat{H}(f))
, we set
\ell\gtrsim k\log(k/\delta)
.

\Delta_{h}
is determined by the parameters of filter
(H(t),\widehat{H}(f))
in Equation (
35
):
\Delta_{h}\eqsim\frac{s_{1}\ell}{s_{3}T}
in section
C.1
.
Combining the setting of
s_{1}
,
s_{3}

\ell
, we should set
\Delta_{h}\geq\widetilde{O}(k^{5}\log(1/\delta))/T
.

k
-cluster Recovery.

Note that in the
k
-cluster recovery, we need to use filter function
(G(t),\widehat{G}(f))
. We choose
l=\log(k/\delta)
,
\alpha\eqsim 1
,
B\eqsim k
, and
D=l/\alpha
.

By proof of Property II of
z
in Lemma
7.20
from section
7.6
, we need
T(1-s_{3})>\sigma Bl
. By the same reason in one-cluster recovery,
1-s_{3}\leq\frac{1}{\widetilde{O}(k^{4})}
. Combining
T(1-s_{3})>\sigma Bl
and
1-s_{3}\leq\frac{1}{\widetilde{O}(k^{4})}
, we obtain

\frac{T}{\widetilde{O}(k^{4})}>\sigma Bl

(36)

Because in our algorithm, we will sample
\sigma
from
[\frac{1}{B\Delta_{h}},\frac{2}{B\Delta_{h}}]
. Thus, plugging
\sigma=\Theta(\frac{1}{B\Delta_{h}})
in Equation (

) we have

\frac{T}{\widetilde{O}(k^{4})}>\frac{l}{\Delta_{h}}

which implies another lower bound for

,

\Delta_{h}\geq\widetilde{O}(k^{4})l/T

Combining the above bound with previous lower bound in one-cluster recovery, we get

\Delta_{h}\geq\widetilde{O}(k^{4}\log(1/\delta))/T+\widetilde{O}(k^{5}\log(1/\delta))/T=\widetilde{O}(k^{5}\log(1/\delta))/T

For
s_{1}
and
\ell
, we still choose the same setting as before,
s_{1}\eqsim\widetilde{O}(k^{4})
and
\ell\eqsim O(k\log(k/\delta))
.

C.4
Analysis of
\texorpdfstring
HashToBins

In this section, we explain the correctness of Procedure
HashToBins
in Algorithm
6
. Before giving the proof of that algorithm, we show how to connect
\mathrm{CFT}
,
\mathrm{DTFT}
and
\mathrm{DFT}
.

Lemma C.12
.

For any signal
W:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, let
A:\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
and
B:[n]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be defined as follows:

\displaystyle A[i]=W(i),\forall i\in\mathbb{Z}\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }B[i]=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}A[i+jn],\forall i\in[n].

Then we consider the Fourier transform on
W,A,
and
B
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{CFT}\quad

\displaystyle\quad\widehat{W}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C},

\displaystyle\mathrm{DTFT}\quad

\displaystyle\quad\widehat{A}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{C},

\displaystyle\mathrm{DFT}\quad

\displaystyle\quad\widehat{B}:[n]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}.

We have:

\forall f\in[0,1),\widehat{A}(f)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{W}(f+j);\quad\forall i\in[n],\widehat{B}[i]=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{W}(i/n+j).

Proof.

Recall that
\Comb_{s}(t)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\delta_{js}(t)
. First, we show
\widehat{A}(f)=\sum_{j\in\Z}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}jf}A[j]
equals to
\underset{j\in\Z}{\sum}\widehat{W}(f+j)
:

\displaystyle\widehat{A}(f)=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\Z}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}jf}W[j]

by
A[j]=W(j)

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}fj}W(j)\cdot\Comb_{1}(j)\mathrm{d}j

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\widehat{W\cdot\Comb_{1}}(f)

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle(\widehat{W}*\widehat{\Comb}_{1})(f)

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\Z}\widehat{W}(f+j).

(37)

Next, we prove that
\forall i\in[n],\widehat{B}[i]=\widehat{A}(i/n)
,

\displaystyle\widehat{B}[i]=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}B[j]e^{\frac{2\pi\mathbf{i}}{n}ij}

by
\mathrm{DFT}

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\sum_{k\in\Z}A[j+kn])e^{\frac{2\pi\mathbf{i}}{n}ij}

by
B[j]=\sum_{k\in\Z}A[j+kn]

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k\in\Z}A[j+kn]e^{\frac{2\pi\mathbf{i}}{n}i(j+kn)}

by
e^{\frac{2\pi\mathbf{i}}{n}\cdot ikn}=1

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\Z}A[j]e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}j\frac{i}{n}}=\widehat{A}(i/n)

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ DTFT}.

(38)

Combining Equation (
C.4
) and Equation (
C.4
), we obtain that
\widehat{B}[j]=\widehat{A}(j/n)=\sum_{i\in\Z}\widehat{W}(j/n+i)
for all
j\in[n]
.
∎

Claim C.13
.

Let
u\in\C^{B}
and
V\in\C^{BD}
such that for any
j\in B
,
u[j]=\underset{i\in[D]}{\sum}V[j+(i-1)B]
. Then

\widehat{u}[j]=\widehat{V}[jD],\forall j\in[B].

Proof.

We prove it through the definition of the Fourier transform:

\displaystyle\widehat{V}[jD]=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{BD}V[i]\cdot e^{\frac{2\pi\mathbf{i}}{BD}\cdot i\cdot(jD)}

by definition of DFT

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{B}\sum_{k=1}^{D}V[i+kB]e^{\frac{2\pi\mathbf{i}}{B}\cdot(i+kB)\cdot j}

by replacing
i
by
i+kB

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{B}e^{\frac{2\pi\mathbf{i}}{B}\cdot j\cdot i}\sum_{k=1}^{D}V[i+(k-1)B]

by
e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}jk}=1

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{B}e^{\frac{2\pi\mathbf{i}}{B}\cdot j\cdot i}u[i]=\widehat{u}[j]

by definition of DFT on
u

∎

We use Definition
6.1
and Lemma
6.2
to generalize Lemma
C.12
,

Corollary C.14
.

If for all
j\in[n]
,
B[j]=\underset{i\in\Z}{\sum}W\big((j+in)\sigma-\sigma a\big)
, then
\forall j\in[n]
,

\widehat{B}[j]=\sum_{i\in\Z}\widehat{W}\left((\frac{j}{n}+i)/\sigma\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma}e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}(\frac{j}{n}+i)a}.

If for all
j\in[n]
,
B[j]=\underset{i\in\Z}{\sum}W\big((j+in)\sigma-\sigma a\big)e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\sigma b(j+in)}
, then
\forall j\in[n]
,

\widehat{B}[j]=\sum_{i\in\Z}\widehat{W}\left((\frac{j}{n}+i)/\sigma+b\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma}e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}(\frac{j}{n}+i)a-2\pi\mathbf{i}\sigma ab}.

Remark C.15

(Samples of
HashToBins
)
.

Procedure
HashToBins
in Algorithm
6
takes
BD
samples in
x(t)
:

x(\sigma(1-a)),x(\sigma(2-a)),\cdots,x(\sigma(BD-a)).

Figure 9:
\widehat{G}_{\sigma,b}^{(j)}(f)
where the top one is
j=0
and the bottom one is
j=1
,
{\cal A}_{i,j}=[\frac{1}{\sigma}(2\pi(i+\frac{j}{B})-\frac{2\pi}{2B}),\frac{1}{\sigma}(2\pi(i+\frac{j}{B})+\frac{2\pi}{2B})]
,
{\cal B}_{i,j}=[\frac{1}{\sigma}(2\pi(i+\frac{j}{B})-\frac{2\pi(1-\alpha)}{2B}),\frac{1}{\sigma}(2\pi(i+\frac{j}{B})+\frac{2\pi(1-\alpha)}{2B})]

To analyze our algorithm, we use filter function
(G(t),\widehat{G}(f))
and
\Comb_{s}(t)=\underset{j\in\Z}{\sum}\delta_{sj}(t)
to define the discretization of
G
.

Definition C.16
.

Define the discretization of
G(t)
and
\widehat{G}(f)
,

\displaystyle G^{\dis}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(t)\cdot\Comb_{s}(t)

\displaystyle\widehat{G}^{\dis}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{s}(\widehat{G}*\Comb_{1/s})(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\widehat{G}*\Comb_{1})(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl({\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[width]{hatG}}}}\bigr)*{\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[width]{comb_1}}}}

where
|\supp(G(t))|=\frac{lB}{\pi\alpha}
,
D=\frac{l}{\pi\alpha}
,
s=|\supp(G(t))|/(BD)=l/(\pi\alpha D)=1
.

Definition
6.8
.

\forall\sigma>0,b
and
j\in[B]
. Define,

\displaystyle G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sigma}G(t/\sigma)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}t(j/B-\sigma b)/\sigma}

\displaystyle\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{G}^{\dis}(\frac{j}{B}-\sigma f-\sigma b)=\sum_{i\in\Z}\widehat{G}(i+\frac{j}{B}-\sigma f-\sigma b)

Lemma
6.9
.

Let
u\in\C^{B}
be the result of
HashToBins
under
permutation
P_{\sigma,a,b}
, and let
j\in[B]
. Define

\widehat{z}=\widehat{x\cdot H}\cdot\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b},

so

z=(x\cdot H)*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}.

Let vector
\widehat{u}\in\C^{B}
denote the
B
-dimensional DFT of
u
, then
\forall j\in[B]
,

\widehat{u}[j]=z_{\sigma a}.

Proof.

Recall
B
is the number of hash bins.
B\cdot D
is the number of samples in time signal.
Let
W(t)=x\cdot H(t)
, define vector
y\in\C^{BD}
, then
\forall j\in[BD]
, define

y[j]=W(\sigma(j-a))e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\sigma bj}

Recall
G(t)
denote the
\rect^{*l}_{B/\alpha}(t)\cdot\sinc(t/B)
, then
|\supp(G(t))|=\frac{lB}{\alpha}
. Let vector
G^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{BD}
is the discretization of
G(t)
, where
G^{\prime}[i]=G(i)
. Then,
\forall j\in[B]
,

u[j]=\sum_{i\in[D]}V[j+iB]

where
V[j]=y[j]\cdot G^{\prime}[j]
and
G^{\prime}[j]
is the value at the
j
th nonzero point of
G^{\dis}(t)
. Applying Claim
C.13
with the definition of
u[j]
and
V[j+iB]
, gives
\widehat{u}[j]=\widehat{V}[jD],\forall j\in[B]
.

Because of
u
is the result of
\textsc{HashToBins}(x\cdot H,P_{\sigma,a,b},G)
and
|\supp(G(t))|=BD
(choosing
D=l/\alpha
), then

u[j]=\sum_{i\in\Z}W(\sigma(j+iB-a))e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\sigma b(j+iB)}G(j+iB)

Then we define
G^{\prime\prime}(t)=G(t/\sigma+a)e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}b\sigma(t/\sigma+a)}
and
Y(t)=W(t)\cdot G^{\prime\prime}(t)
, then immediately, we have

\widehat{G}^{\prime\prime}(f)=\sigma\widehat{G}(\sigma(f-b))e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}a\sigma f}\quad\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\quad\widehat{Y}(f)=\widehat{W}(f)*\widehat{G}^{\prime\prime}(f)

Thus, we can rewrite
u[j]
in the following sense,

\displaystyle u[j]

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\Z}W(\sigma(j+iB-a))e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\sigma b(j+iB)}G(j+iB)

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\Z}W(\sigma(j+iB-a))G^{\prime\prime}(\sigma(j+iB-a))

by
G^{\prime\prime}(t)=G(t/\sigma+a)e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}b\sigma(t/\sigma+a)}

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\Z}Y(\sigma(j+iB-a))

by
Y(t)=W(t)\cdot G^{\prime\prime}(t)

Then

\displaystyle\widehat{u}[j]

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\Z}\widehat{Y}((\frac{j}{B}+i)/\sigma)\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma}\cdot e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}(\frac{j}{B}+i)a}

by Corollary
C.14

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\Z}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\widehat{W}(s)\cdot\widehat{G}^{\prime\prime}(\frac{j/B+i}{\sigma}-s)\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma}\cdot e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\cdot(j/B+i)a}\mathrm{d}s

by
\widehat{Y}(f)=\widehat{W}(f)*\widehat{G}^{\prime\prime}(f)

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\Z}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\widehat{W}(s)\cdot\widehat{G}(j/B+i-\sigma s-\sigma b)\cdot e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}\cdot(-\sigma s)a}\mathrm{d}s

by
\widehat{G}^{\prime\prime}(f)=\sigma\widehat{G}(\sigma(f-b))e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}a\sigma f}

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\widehat{W}(s)\cdot\sum_{i\in\Z}\widehat{G}((j/B+i)-\sigma s-\sigma b)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}a\sigma s}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\widehat{W}(s)\cdot\widehat{G}^{\dis}(\frac{j}{B}-\sigma s-\sigma b)e^{-2\pi\mathbf{i}a\sigma s}\mathrm{d}s

by
\widehat{G}^{\dis}(f)=\sum_{i\in\Z}\widehat{G}(f+i)

By definition
C.16
,

\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}=\widehat{G}^{\dis}(\frac{j}{B}-\sigma s-\sigma b)=\sum_{i\in\Z}\widehat{G}(i+\frac{j}{B}-\sigma s-\sigma b)

By definition of
\widehat{z}
, we have

\widehat{z}(s)=\widehat{x\cdot H}(s)\cdot\widehat{G}^{(j)}(s)=\widehat{W}(s)\cdot\widehat{G}^{(j)}(s)

Then
\widehat{u}[j]
is the
(a\sigma)^{th}
inverse Fourier coefficients of
\widehat{z}
, basically,

\widehat{u}[j]=z_{a\sigma}=z(a\sigma)

Thus, we can conclude first computing vector
u\in\mathbb{C}^{B}
. Getting vector
\widehat{u}\in\mathbb{C}^{B}
by using the Discrete Fourier transform
\widehat{u}=\mathrm{DFT}(u)
. This procedure allows us to sample from time domain to implicitly access the time signal’s Fourier transform
\widehat{z}
. If
z
is one-cluster in frequency domain, then apply one-cluster recovery algorithm.

∎

Appendix D
